\chapter*{Preface}\label{preface}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Preface}

\markboth{Preface}{Preface}

This notebook was created to host learning materials for Math 130:
Introduction to R at California State University, Chico.

From the course catalog:

\begin{quote}
\emph{This accelerated short course is designed as a primer to get the
complete novice up and running with basic knowledge of how to use the
statistical programming language R. The target audience is anyone who
wants to become the boss of their data and conduct analyses. The course
covers getting data into R, manipulating data into an analyzable format,
and creating informative plots. Emphasis is placed on reproducibility
and literate programming. The course culminates with a data exploration
project.}
\end{quote}

Math 130 is designed as a short-course ``primer'' to be taken before, or
concurrently with, an upper division or graduate Statistics course such
as Math 315 or Math 615. This can also be used a prepratory learning for
classified admission into the
\href{https://www.csuchico.edu/math/degrees/msdsa.shtml}{Masters in Data
Science and Analytics Program}{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}Want to
Learn Python? Check out Math 131 \end{footnotesize}}}

The goal of Math 130 is to get the complete novice up and running with
the basic knowledge of how to use the statistical programming language R
in an environment that emphasizes reproducible research and literate
programming for data analysis. We recognize there are many topics and
many approaches to teach an introduction to the R programming language.
We have picked specific topics and methods that we believe learners will
need to succeed in subsequent classes.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Course Learning Objectives}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

By the end of this class you will be able to:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Import spreadsheet-like data into R from an external file
\item
  Perform basic data wrangling like handling missing values, creating
  new variables, labeling categorical variables, and filtering rows.
\item
  Create statistical data visualizations such as barcharts, histograms,
  boxplots, scatterplots, and extend to multivariate data by modifying
  colors, sizes, and panels.
\item
  Create statistical data summaries such as tables, proportions, and
  summary statistics.
\item
  Perform all of this work in a reproducible manner using professional
  tools.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\markboth{Course Overview}{Course Overview}

\section*{Program Terminology}\label{program-terminology}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Program Terminology}

\markright{Program Terminology}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The term \textbf{R} refers to both the programming language and the
  software that interprets the scripts written using it.
\item
  An \textbf{IDE} is an ``Integrated Development Environment'' and
  essentially is another program that lets you interact with R in a
  productive and efficient manner.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{\href{https://posit.co/downloads/}{RStudio}} has been a very
    popular IDE since around 2011 that has continued to flourish and
    improve throughtout the years.
  \item
    \textbf{\href{https://positron.posit.co/}{Positron}} is a new IDE
    created by the developers of RStudio that is based off another
    popular IDE called VSCode - and aimed primarily at Data Scientists
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

This book teaches you how to use R from within the \textbf{R Studio} IDE
as we feel it is easier for new learners to start with. You will have to
install both onto your computer. Setup instructions are discussed later
in Section Section~\ref{sec-install}.

\section*{Motivation}\label{motivation}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Motivation}

\markright{Motivation}

\subsection*{Why use R?}\label{why-use-r}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Why use R?}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Open source, cross-platform, and free
\item
  Great for reproducibility
\item
  Tons of learning resources
\item
  Works on data of all shapes and sizes
\item
  Produces high-quality graphics
\item
  Large and welcoming community
\item
  Flexible and extensible. Doesn't do something you want? Create a
  custom function for yourself.
\item
  Currently R is used in many upper division \& graduate level
  Statistics and Research methods courses in domains such as Public
  Health, Economics, Finance, and Political Science.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/r-manual.jpeg}}

}

\caption{Manual car gear shift representing coding in R Photo credit}

\end{figure}%

\subsection*{Why use R Studio?}\label{why-use-r-studio}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Why use R Studio?}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Customizable workspace that docks all windows together.
\item
  Notebook formats that allow for easy sharing of code and output, and
  integration with other languages (Python, C++, SQL, Stan)
\item
  Syntax highlighting, warning errors when missing a closing
  parentheses.
\item
  Cross-platform interface. Also works on Windows/iOS/Linux.
\item
  Tab completion for functions. Forget the syntax or a variable name?
  Popup helpers are available.
\item
  One button publishing of
  \href{https://quarto.org/docs/gallery/}{reproducible documents} such
  as reports, dashboards, presentations, websites like this one!
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/r-automatic.jpeg}}

}

\caption{Automatic car gear shift representing RStudio integrated
development environment Photo credit}

\end{figure}%

\section*{Programming can be scary!}\label{programming-can-be-scary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Programming can be scary!}

\markright{Programming can be scary!}

It's important to acknowledge that computer programming is a new skill,
that may bring a roller coaster of emotions with it while you learn.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \includegraphics[width=6.25in,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{img/r-learning-curve-horst.png}

}

\caption{Learning curve diagram by Allison Horst showing how much you
think you know about R over time}

\end{figure}%

{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}Artwork by @allison\_horst -- used
under CC BY 4.0.\end{footnotesize}}}

But you don't have to go at it alone! You are now part of a
\emph{learning community} that you can lean on for questions and
support.

\includegraphics[width=6.25in,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{img/r-community.png}
{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}Drop in help available at Community
Coding\end{footnotesize}}}

Learning to program has lots of benefits

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Improves your logical skills and critical problem solving
\item
  Increases your attention to detail
\item
  Increases your self reliance and empowers you to control your own
  research.
\item
  Your PI will love your awesome graphics and reports.
\item
  Some people think what you do is magic.
\item
  Thinking graduate school?
  \href{http://www.nature.com/nature/journal/v541/n7638/full/nj7638-563a.html}{expect
  to learn this on your own}
\item
  \href{https://skillcrush.com/2015/01/28/laurence-bradford-10-reasons/}{A
  few}
  \href{https://careerfoundry.com/en/blog/web-development/7-benefits-of-learning-to-code/}{other
  lists}
  \href{https://skillcrush.com/2017/01/30/learn-to-code-benefits/}{of
  reasons}
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Why no point and click?}\label{why-no-point-and-click}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Why no point and click?}

Because it's not reproducible.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Which boxes did you click last time?
\item
  New data? Gotta do it all over.
\item
  Need to expand your model? Gotta do it all over.
\item
  Made a mistake in the data coding? Gotta do it all over\ldots{}
\end{itemize}

🚧 Section under construction

\subsection*{Code and keystroke
appearance}\label{code-and-keystroke-appearance}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Code and keystroke appearance}

In these notes, code that is referenced in text is
\texttt{displayed\ in\ this\ format}, code is executed (run) by R is
displayed like this:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{+}\DecValTok{2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 4
\end{verbatim}

where the output or result of the code is displayed directly after. The
\texttt{{[}1{]}} that is sometimes displayed with the output helps count
the lines of output (sometimes), and can be ignored \emph{most} of the
time.

When giving instructions on which keys to press, buttons are shown as
boxes like CTRL or ENTER .

Assignments}\label{getting-and-submitting-assignments}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Getting and Submitting Assignments}

\part{Getting Started}

\chapter{Your New Tools}\label{your-new-tools}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Learning Objectives}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

After completing this lesson learners will be able to:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Install and access R and R Studio.
\item
  Navigate the RStudio GUI.
\item
  Install additional packages using R code.
\item
  Create an organized class folder.
\item
  Create and use an R Project.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Installing R and R Studio}\label{sec-install}

Follow the instructions appropriate for your operating system.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, rightrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, left=2mm, toprule=.15mm, opacityback=0, colback=white]

\vspace{-3mm}\textbf{Tablet / iPad / Chromebook}\vspace{3mm}

If you are using a tablet, Chromebook or otherwise do not have a
computer that you can install programs on, you can use Posit Cloud for
this class.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Make a \textbf{Cloud FREE} account at
  \url{https://posit.cloud/plans/free}
\item
  Start a new project by clicking on the button in the top right corner
\item
  Then go to Section~\ref{sec-nav_Rstudio} to learn how to navigate
  RStudio.
\end{itemize}

The free account allows for 25 project hours/month, which may not be
enough for this class. If you run into time limits or you are using this
for another class you will have to upgrade to the \textbf{Cloud Student}
plan which is \$5/month.

Using the cloud is easier to initially setup, but having your own
installation on your computer ensures that

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  you want to keep the program free forever
\item
  you will be able to put your files under version control
\item
  you always have access to your code even with unstable or no internet
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, rightrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, left=2mm, toprule=.15mm, opacityback=0, colback=white]

\vspace{-3mm}\textbf{Windows}\vspace{3mm}

\textbf{Install R}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Go to \url{https://cran.r-project.org/bin/windows/base/} and download
  the latest \textbf{R installer}.
\item
  Double-click the downloaded file and follow the prompts (default
  settings are fine).
\item
  When finished, you can delete the desktop shortcut that looks like a
  blue R.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Install RStudio}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Go to \url{https://posit.co/download/rstudio-desktop/} and download
  \textbf{RStudio Desktop (Free)} for Windows.
\item
  Double-click the installer and follow the prompts.
\item
  When installation finishes, open \textbf{RStudio} --- it should detect
  R automatically.
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, rightrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, left=2mm, toprule=.15mm, opacityback=0, colback=white]

\vspace{-3mm}\textbf{MacOS}\vspace{3mm}

\textbf{Install R}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Go to \url{https://cran.r-project.org/bin/macosx/} and download the
  latest \textbf{.pkg} installer (Intel or Apple Silicon).
\item
  Double-click to install R.
\item
  If prompted to install \textbf{XQuartz}, use the link on that page to
  complete the setup.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Install RStudio}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}

\item
  Go to \url{https://posit.co/download/rstudio-desktop/} and download
  \textbf{RStudio Desktop (Free)} for macOS.
\item
  Drag the \textbf{RStudio} icon into your \textbf{Applications} folder.
\item
  After installation, eject the downloaded drive by dragging it to
  \textbf{Trash}, then open RStudio from Applications.

\section{Terminology}\label{terminology}

The basis of programming is that we write down instructions for the
computer to follow, and then we tell the computer to follow those
instructions.

We write, or \emph{code}, instructions in R because it is a common
language that both the computer and we can understand (with practice).

We call the instructions \emph{commands} and we tell the computer to
follow the instructions by \emph{executing} (also called \emph{running})
those commands.

Learning how to code is like learning a new language. There are rules
and exceptions, and it can be extremely precise and sometimes
frustrating in how literal it is.

{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}Writing clear commands can be like
explaining how to make a PB\&J sandwich to a child.\end{footnotesize}}}
\url{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cDA3_5982h8}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, rightrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, left=2mm, toprule=.15mm, opacityback=0, colback=white]

This class does not assume that you have any knowledge in programming,
and will provide all the codes that you need. For best results you
should be trying to understand this new language, and how to tell R to
do what you want it to do.

The tendency may be to reach out to AI to help write code for you, but
this does not help you \emph{learn} and worse can provide code that is
overly complicated and does not work as intended.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Navigating R Studio}\label{sec-nav_Rstudio}

We will be interacting with the programming language R \emph{only}
through R Studio. Not by itself. Typically are four panes, or windows,
in R Studio. When you first start the program, you may only see three.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{index_files/mediabag/rstudio_session_4pan.png}}

}

\caption{The major windows (or panes) of the RStudio environment:}

\end{figure}%

{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}Details pulled from the Data Carpentry
Genomics lesson\end{footnotesize}}}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-caution-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-caution-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={Optional video}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\href{https://youtu.be/vx4SPeNvols?si=1iSqsH1uNLFkB7yF&t=239}{Navigating
R Studio by R Girls on YouTube}

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsubsection*{Console/Terminal/Jobs}\label{consoleterminaljobs}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Console/Terminal/Jobs}

The \textbf{console} (left side) is where you see the execution of
commands. You can type commands directly into the console and press
Enter to execute those commands.

\textbf{R is an overgrown calculator}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

In the console type the following code, then press Enter.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{+}\DecValTok{2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 4
\end{verbatim}

\end{tcolorbox}

Once these commands are submitted (the code is run) they will be
forgotten when you close the session. To save your commands you'll want
to write them in a \emph{script} file.

\subsubsection*{Source}\label{source}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Source}

This pane is where you will write/view R and Quarto \emph{scripts}. Some
outputs (such as a dataset) will appear as a tab here.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\emph{Click on \textbf{File --\textgreater{} New File --\textgreater{} R
Script}} to open an R script file.

\end{tcolorbox}

You should now have four panes, with a new \texttt{untitled1.R} script
file in the top left. When you write R code here you can save these
commands, and rerun them later on demand.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

In the script file type \texttt{2+2} and then then press CTRL+Enter
while your cursor is still on the same line.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{+}\DecValTok{2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 4
\end{verbatim}

\end{tcolorbox}

You will see the command echoed down in the Console along with the
answer. We will come back to using scripts in the next lesson.

\subsubsection*{Environment / History}\label{environment-history}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Environment / History}

The \textbf{Environment / History} pane (top-right) shows all the
objects you've created in your current R session --- for example, data
frames, variables, or plots you've assigned.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The \textbf{Environment} tab lists everything that currently exists in
  memory. This could be data sets, model results and defined variables.
\end{itemize}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

In console run \texttt{data(cars)}. This loads a built-in data set named
\texttt{cars} into your environment. Click on the word \texttt{cars} in
the top right, and notice it opens the data set in a spreadsheet like
view in the Source pane.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The \textbf{History} tab keeps a record of all the commands you've
  run.
\end{itemize}

There are also some additional tabs in this area, like
\textbf{Connections}, \textbf{Build}, \textbf{Tutorial}, and sometimes
\textbf{Git}. We won't cover those here, but RStudio has many additional
tools and features available as you learn more.

\subsubsection*{Files / Plots / Packages /
Help}\label{files-plots-packages-help}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Files / Plots / Packages / Help}

The \textbf{Files / Plots / Packages / Help} pane is used to explore
files, view plots, manage packages, and read help pages.

\section{Customizing}\label{customizing}

Setting preferences in R Studio

{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}AKA: Retain sanity while
troubleshooting\end{footnotesize}}}

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{img/rstudio_settings.png}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Open \textbf{RStudio} and go to \textbf{Tools → Global
  Options\ldots{}}\\
\item
  Uncheck \textbf{``Restore .RData into workspace at startup.''}\\
\item
  For \textbf{``Save workspace to .RData on exit:''} choose
  \textbf{Never.}\\
\item
  Click \textbf{Apply}, then \textbf{OK} to close the window.
\end{enumerate}

This ensures that each time you restart R, you start with a clean
environment instead of carrying forward objects from a previous session.

\section{Packages}\label{sec-packages}

{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}``Where the real money from the movie
is made.''\end{footnotesize}}}

R is considered an \textbf{Open Source} software program. That means
many (thousands) of people contribute to the software. They do this by
writing commands (called functions) to make a particular analysis
easier, or to make a graphic prettier.

When you download R, you get access to a lot of functions that we will
use. However these other \emph{user-written} packages add so much good
stuff that it really is the backbone of the customizability and
functionality that makes R so powerful of a language.

For example we will be creating graphics using functions like
\texttt{boxplot()} and \texttt{hist()} that exist in base R. But we will
quickly move on to creating graphics using functions contained in the
\texttt{ggplot2} package. We will be managing data using functions in
\texttt{dplyr} and reading in Excel files using \texttt{readxl}.
Installing packages will become your favorite past-time.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Install \texttt{ggplot2}}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Typing the following in the console to install the \texttt{ggplot2}
package.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{install.packages}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"ggplot2"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{tcolorbox}

When the download and install is complete, you should see a message
similar to:

\begin{verbatim}
The downloaded binary packages are in
    C:\Users\Robin\AppData\Local\Temp\Rtmpi8NAym\downloaded_packages
\end{verbatim}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{R is Case sensitive}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

R is case sensitive and spelling matters. If you get an error message
like the following.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Warning }\ControlFlowTok{in}\NormalTok{ install.packages }\SpecialCharTok{:}
\NormalTok{  package ‘ggplot’ is not }\FunctionTok{available}\NormalTok{ (}\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ R version }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{.}\FloatTok{5.1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The correct package name is \texttt{ggplot2}, not \texttt{ggplot}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Alternative Method of installing Packages}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Additional packages can be installed from the `packages' tab.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  On the packages tab, click the `Install' icon and start typing the
  name of the package you want in the text box.
\item
  At the bottom of the Install Packages window is a check box to
  `Install' dependencies. This is ticked by default, which is usually
  what you want. Packages can (and do) make use of functionality built
  into other packages, so for the functionality contained in the package
  you are installing to work properly, there may be other packages which
  have to be installed with them. The `Install dependencies' option
  makes sure that this happens.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Install all the things!}

\includegraphics[width=5.20833in,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{img/allthethings.jpg}

Now that you're a package installing pro, go ahead and install the
following packages that we will be using in the next few weeks.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Install the list of packages to the left one at a time and wait for it

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Trust but Verify}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Check to see if you have a package installed by looking in the packages
tab. You can also type the command \texttt{installed.packages()} into
the console and examine the output.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{A note about the \texttt{tidyverse}.}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\emph{``The tidyverse is an opinionated collection of R packages
designed for data science. All packages share an underlying design
philosophy, grammar, and data structures.''}

We will specifically be using the \texttt{dplyr}, \texttt{ggplot2} and
\texttt{readxl} packages that are part of, and thus installed with, the
\texttt{tidyverse}. In these lessons we will load the specific package
that we need (e.g.~only \texttt{dplyr} or only \texttt{ggplot2}), but
loading the \texttt{tidyverse} has the same effect of readying the
necessary functions as loading each individual package. Learn more about
the \href{https://tidyverse.org/}{tidyverse}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Get oRganized!}\label{get-organized}

It is good practice to keep a set of related data, analyses, and text
self-contained in a single folder called the \emph{working directory}.
For example keeping all files related to one class in it's own folder,
separate from other classes. If you are taking another class using R at
the same time as this one, it's even MORE important to keep your files
separate.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Important}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

THIS SHOULD NOT BE YOUR DOWNLOADS FOLDER

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Names are important}\label{names-are-important}

Choose one of the four naming convention shown below for your class
folder and stick with it. These are \texttt{snake\_case},
\texttt{camelCase}, \texttt{kebab-case} or \texttt{UPPER\_SNAKE}.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \includegraphics[width=6.25in,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{img/name_things.png}

}

\caption{Common Naming Conventions}

\end{figure}%

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{Create your folders}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  On your computer, in an easy to find place, create a new folder named
  \texttt{math130} using your chosen naming convention.
\item
  Then create three subfolders: \texttt{data}, \texttt{homework},
  \texttt{notes}.
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{center}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/folder_org.PNG}}
\end{center}

\subsection{Be the boss of your files}\label{be-the-boss-of-your-files}

When you download a file, right click and ``Save as'' or ``Save target
as'' and \textbf{actively choose} where to download this file.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Avoid these pain points}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Do not left click on the links
\item
  Do not let files live in your downloads folder.
\item
  Do not open any files from your browser window after downloading.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Right click
\href{https://quarto.org/docs/get-started/hello/rstudio/_hello.qmd}{{[}this
link{]}} to download and save this file into your \texttt{homework}
folder. We will use this file in the next lesson.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Organization using R Projects}\label{sec-rproj}

R projects are a great way of keeping all files for one project all
together, and makes importing data much easier by using relative paths
instead of absolute ones. Plus this ensures reproducibility by others
(because no-one else stores their files at
\texttt{C:\textbackslash{}users\textbackslash{}rdonatello\textbackslash{}myprojects\textbackslash{}math130})

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Click on the R cube icon in the top right corner of RStudio and select
  \emph{Crete new project}.
\item
  Choose \emph{Existing Directory} and navigate to your Math 130 folder.
\item
  Check \emph{Open in new session}. This allows you to have multiple
  Rstudio windows open for different projects and the files/data/objects
  don't get cross-contaminated.
\item
  Click \emph{Create Project}.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

This will open a new R Studio window. Go ahead and close both R Studio
windows for now (Don't save \texttt{Untitled1.R}). We'll see how to use
R Projects next.

If you think about wanting to work on a report written in Microsoft
Word, or a video that you want to edit, the common practice is to
navigate to that file and double click to open that file in the
appropriate program. We don't tend to open the program first, and then
try to open the file from within the program. Using R Projects is
somewhat the same.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Using R Projects}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Navigate in your file explorer to the folder where you created your
  project.
\item
  Click on the R cube icon to open the whole project.
  \includegraphics[width=0.1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{img/Rproj_cube.png}
\item
  Using the \texttt{files} tab (bottom right) find and click to open the
  \texttt{hello.qmd} script file you downloaded earlier.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

You can tell you are in an R Project by looking in the top right corner
by the Environment pane and seeing if the R project cube has your class
folder name

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/RProj.png}}

}

\caption{Example R Project}

\section{Final Tips}\label{final-tips}

\subsubsection{Saving and closing your
work}\label{saving-and-closing-your-work}

Unless you're returning to work in R Studio in a short while, you should
make a habit to save all open tabs and completely shut down R studio
when you are done working. This ensures your environment is cleared.
{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}This is a good
thing\end{footnotesize}}}

\subsubsection{Restart R}\label{restart-r}

To restart R without shutting the entire window down, go to the file
menu bar in the top,

\begin{quote}
\emph{Session} --\textgreater{} Restart R and Clear Output
\end{quote}

This is good to do when switching between projects/classes.

\chapter{Reproducibility with Quarto}\label{reproducibility-with-quarto}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/quarto.png}}

}

\caption{A schematic representing the multi-language input (e.g.~Python,
R, Observable, Julia) and multi-format output (e.g.~PDF, html, Word
documents, and more) versatility of Quarto.}

\end{figure}%

\href{https://quarto.org/}{Quarto} is a versatile publishing system that
allows users to create high-quality documents, reports, websites (like
this one!) and presentations using multiple programming languages like
R, Python, and Julia. It combines the functionalities of various tools
into a single platform, making it easier to produce reproducible and
collaborative scientific content. If you have heard of R Markdown -
Quarto is the ``next generation'' of R Markdown.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={🎓 Learning Objectives}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

After completing this lesson learners will be able to:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Write code and text in a Quarto document
\item
  Identify components of a Quarto document
\item
  Compile Quarto documents into both HTML and PDF formats
\item
  Understand the difference between running code interactively, and
  rendering an entire new document.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

While Quarto is also it's own standalone program, it comes ``built-in''
to R Studio so {\textbf{no additional installations needed!}}

\section{Document structure}\label{document-structure}

Quarto is an example of literate programming where the explanation of
the program (or analysis) logic is presented in a natural language (such
as English), with supporting pieces of code embedded in the document
itself. Quarto combines normal text such as this sentence, code and the
output from the code all in one file. It is similar to a scientific lab
notebook where a scientist can write down their hypotheses, code to
produce graphs and analysis results, and the discussion of those
findings.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{index_files/mediabag/rstudio-hello.png}}

}

\caption{A Quarto document with the extension .qmd (on the left), along
with its rendered version as HTML (on the right). Credit: Quarto
documentation.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, rightrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, left=2mm, toprule=.15mm, opacityback=0, colback=white]

\vspace{-3mm}\textbf{Optional Video}\vspace{3mm}

\href{https://youtu.be/_f3latmOhew}{Getting started with Quarto}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Open the Quarto tutorial file}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Make sure you are in your Math 130 Project. (ref
  Section~\ref{sec-rproj})
\item
  From the file pane, open the \texttt{\_hello.qmd} quarto document
\item
  Click ``Render'' at the top of this file.
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

The
\includegraphics[width=0.26042in,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{img/rstudio-render-button.png}
Render button in the RStudio IDE \emph{compiles} or \emph{renders} the
file and opens a preview of the output with a single click or keyboard
shortcut SHIFT+COMMAND+K.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/rstudio-render.png}}

}

\caption{The Render button}

\end{figure}%

\section{Components of a Quarto file}\label{sec-quarto-components}

Quarto files contains three types of content:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  A YAML header
\item
  Code chunks
\item
  Markdown text
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{YAML Header}\label{yaml-header}

An YAML header is demarcated by three dashes (---) on top and bottom. R
is pretty finicky about these three dashes, so try not to disturb them.

When rendered, the title, \texttt{"Hello,\ Quarto"}, will appear at the
top of the rendered document with a larger font size than the rest of
the document. The other two YAML fields denote that the output should be
in \texttt{html\ format} and the document should open in the
\texttt{visual\ editor} by default.

The basic syntax of YAML uses key-value pairs in the format
\texttt{key:\ value}. Other YAML fields commonly found in headers of
documents include metadata like \texttt{author:} and \texttt{date:}.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Add your name}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Add a line after the title for an \texttt{author:} and put your name
  here.
\item
  Click the ``Source'' button at the top left corner of the
  \texttt{Source} window.
\item
  Render the document again.
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Visual vs Source mode} RStudio's \textbf{Visual} editor offers a
\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WYSIWYM}{WYSIWYM}
{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}WYSIWYM: What You See Is What You
Mean\end{footnotesize}}} authoring experience for markdown formatting of
text. Each view has it's benefits and drawbacks, and which you choose to
use is purely a matter of personal preference.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{index_files/mediabag/rstudio-source-visua.png}}

}

\caption{The same Quarto document in the two modes of the RStudio
editor: visual (on the left) and source (on the right).}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Code Chunks}\label{code-chunks}

Code chunks start with three back ticks
\texttt{\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}}
{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}a back tick is the key to the left of
the 1 on your keyboard\end{footnotesize}}} and an \texttt{\{r\}} in
braces. Chunks close (end) with another three back ticks.
{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}Note the background color of this
section has changed to a different shade. This helps you identify you
have closed your code chunk properly.\end{footnotesize}}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\InformationTok{\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}\{r\}}
\InformationTok{\#| eval: false}
\InformationTok{2+2}
\InformationTok{\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  You can insert code chunks by using the button in the top right of an
  RMD file (Insert -\textgreater{} R), or by typing CTRL+ALT+I.
\item
  R code chunks can also have a YAML style header, prefixed by a
  ``hash-pipe'' \texttt{\#\textbar{}} at the top of the code chunk.
  {\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}\texttt{\textbar{}} is the first of
  three pipes we'll see in this course\end{footnotesize}}} In this case
  I set \texttt{eval} to \texttt{false} to prevent the code inside this
  code chunk from being run when I render the whole document.
\end{itemize}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Only code goes in code chunks}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

That's why they're called \texttt{code} chunks. No normal text. All
explanatory text goes outside code chunks in normal Markdown text.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Make a change}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Play around with modifying the text and the R code.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Change \texttt{echo:false} to \texttt{echo:true} in the
  \texttt{plot-penguins} code chunk header.
\item
  Type \texttt{colors()} in your console to pull up all the named colors
  in R. Change at least one color in the penguin plot. You can find them
  in the code line \texttt{values\ =\ c("darkorange","purple","cyan4")}
\item
  Write ``Hello Penguins!'' on line 45, at least one blank line after
  the the end of the code chunk and your sentence.
\item
  Render this document and observe your changes.
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

In addition to rendering the complete document to view the results of
code chunks, you can also run each code chunk interactively in the
RStudio editor by clicking the play button icon in the top right corner
of the code chunk, or keyboard shortcut CTRL + SHIFT + ENTER. RStudio
executes the code and displays the results either inline within your
file or in the Console, depending on your preference.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Add some code}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Insert a new code chunk after your ``Hello Penguins!'' sentence. Make
  sure there is a blank line between your sentence and the code chunk
\item
  Type the code \texttt{2+2} in this code chunk and click the play
  button to execute it immediately.\\
\item
  Render your document and observe your changes.
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Markdown Text}\label{sec-markdown}

Markdown is a popular markup language that allows you to add formatting
elements to text. Markdown is useful because it is lightweight,
flexible, and platform independent. {[}See more basic syntax of Markdown
at https://quarto.org/docs/authoring/markdown-basics.html{]}\{.aside\}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
What you type & What you see \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{**bold**} & \textbf{bold} \\
\texttt{\_italics\_} & \emph{italics} \\
\texttt{\textasciigrave{}code\textasciigrave{}} & \texttt{code} \\
\texttt{\$2\textbackslash{}beta\ -\ \textbackslash{}frac\{\textbackslash{}mu\}\{n\}\$}
& \(2\beta - \frac{\mu}{n}\) \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2778}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2639}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
What you type
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
What you see
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\begin{minipage}[t]{\linewidth}\centering
\begin{verbatim}
1.  one
2.  two
\end{verbatim}
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{\linewidth}\centering
\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  one
\item
  two
\end{enumerate}
\end{minipage} \\
\begin{minipage}[t]{\linewidth}\centering
\begin{verbatim}
*  bullet
*  points
\end{verbatim}
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{\linewidth}\centering
\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  bullet
\item
  points
\end{itemize}
\end{minipage} \\
\end{longtable}

Additionally, using \texttt{\#} and \texttt{\#\#} symbols at the start
of a line creates first (\texttt{\#}) and second (\texttt{\#\#}) level
headers (larger font size, bold, numbered), such as
Section~\ref{sec-quarto-components} and line 15 in the
\texttt{hello.qmd} file where it says \texttt{\#\#\ Meet\ Quarto} .
Properly formatted headers are an important part of document
organization and accessibility for screen reader navigation.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Add some formatting}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Add some formatting to your ``Hello Penguins!'' sentence.
\item
  Render your document and observe your changes.
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Create professional looking PDF
reports.}\label{create-professional-looking-pdf-reports.}

To convert your work into a professional looking PDF, or to write math
symbols in your homework, or you need a typesetting program called LaTeX
(pronounced ``lay-tek'' or ``lah-tex''). It's a super neat program, but
also nearly 4Gb. Too big for our needs. That's where the
\texttt{tinytex} package came from. we're going to use it to install a
lightweight version of LaTeX. This will let us write math equations in
Markdown/Quarto, and create nice PDF's of your work.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  In the \emph{console} type \texttt{tinytex::install\_tinytex()} and
  press Enter. This may take a few minutes to fully install.
\item
  In the YAML header change \texttt{format:\ html} to
  \texttt{format:\ pdf}
\item
  Render your document again.
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

\chapter{Getting Started with R}\label{sec-intro_r}

This lesson is designed to explain the basics of how R works as a
programming language.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={🎓 Learning Objectives}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Define the following terms as they relate to R: object, assign, call,
  function, arguments, options.
\item
  Assign values to objects in R.
\item
  Learn how to \emph{name} objects
\item
  Solve simple arithmetic operations in R.
\item
  Call functions and use arguments to change their default options.
\item
  Inspect the content of vectors and manipulate their content.
\item
  Subset and extract values from vectors.
\item
  Write logical statements that resolve as TRUE and FALSE.
\item
  Describe what a data frame is.
\item
  Summarize the contents of a data frame.
\item
  Extract vectors out of data frames using variable names.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Prepare}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Open your Math 130 R Project.
\item
  Right click and ``save as'' this lessons
  \href{notes/intro_r_notes.qmd}{{[}Quarto notes file{]}} and save into
  your \texttt{Math130/notes} folder.
\item
  In the \emph{Files} pane, open this Quarto file and Render this file.
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{R as a calculator}\label{r-as-a-calculator}

In the last lesson you saw how to do basic math in the console. Now try
a more complicated equation.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Type this into the console \emph{exactly} as it is written below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{5}\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{8}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{3}\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{log10}\ErrorTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Error in parse(text = input): <text>:1:21: unexpected ')'
1: 2 + 5*(8^3)- 3*log10)
                        ^
\end{verbatim}

\end{tcolorbox}

Uh oh, we got an Error. Nothing to worry about, errors happen all the
time.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Put a open parenthesis \texttt{(} before \texttt{log10} to fix it and
try again.

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{\textgreater{} R is waiting on you\ldots{}}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Run the following code in the console.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{5}\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{8}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{3}\SpecialCharTok{*}\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{tcolorbox}

Notice the console shows a \texttt{+} prompt. This means that you
haven't finished entering a complete command.

This is because you have not `closed' a parenthesis or quotation,
i.e.~you don't have the same number of left-parentheses as
right-parentheses, or the same number of opening and closing quotation
marks.

When this happens, and you thought you finished typing your command,
click inside the console window and press Esc; this will cancel the
incomplete command and return you to the \texttt{\textgreater{}} prompt.

Let's go back to that long expression but this time type it into a new
code chunk. Recall we can make a new code chunk by pressing
\texttt{CTRL}+\texttt{ALT}+\texttt{I}, or by clicking on \emph{Insert}
then \emph{R}. Also recall that we submit this code by pressing
\texttt{Ctrl}+\texttt{Enter} or clicking the green play arrow in the top
right corner of the code chunk.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Type the following into a new code chunk in your notes, fix the mistake
and run the code chunk

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{5}\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{8}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{3}\SpecialCharTok{*}\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{tcolorbox}

As you go through these lessons, follow along in your own notes file.
Filling in blanks and/or retype each code chunk. Think of this as taking
notes off the chalkboard while the instructor teaches - it's important
that you annotate these notes as you would take notes in any other
class. For you to retain what you are reading and learning, writing out
what these pieces of code are doing (e.g.~the assignment operator
\texttt{\textless{}-}) in \textbf{your own words} is an effective
learning technique.

\section{Creating objects in R}\label{creating-objects-in-r}

To do useful and interesting things, we need to assign \emph{values} to
\emph{objects}. To create an object, we need to give it a name followed
by the assignment operator \texttt{\textless{}-}, and the value we want
to give it:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{weight\_kg }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{55}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{\textless{}-} is the assignment operator. It assigns values on
the right to objects on the left. So, after executing
\texttt{x\ \textless{}-\ 3}, the value of \texttt{x} is \texttt{3}.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Names are important}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Objects can be given any name such as \texttt{x},
\texttt{current\_temperature}, or \texttt{subject\_id}. However there
are some naming guidelines you need to be aware of.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  You want your object names to be explicit and not too long.
\item
  They cannot start with a number (\texttt{2x} is not valid, but
  \texttt{x2} is).
\item
  R is case sensitive (e.g., \texttt{weight\_kg} is different from
  \texttt{Weight\_kg}).
\item
  There are some names that cannot be used because they are the names of
  fundamental functions in R (e.g., \texttt{if}, \texttt{else},
  \texttt{for}, see
  \href{https://stat.ethz.ch/R-manual/R-devel/library/base/html/Reserved.html}{here}
  for a complete list).
\item
  It's best to not use other function names (e.g., \texttt{c},
  \texttt{T}, \texttt{mean}, \texttt{data}, \texttt{df},
  \texttt{weights}) because these already tend to be in use by different
  parts of R.
\item
  See \href{https://google.github.io/styleguide/Rguide.xml}{Google's}
  style guide for more information.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

When assigning a value to an object, R does not print anything. You can
force R to print the value by using parentheses or by typing the object
name:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{weight\_kg }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{55}    \CommentTok{\# doesn\textquotesingle{}t print anything}
\NormalTok{(weight\_kg }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{55}\NormalTok{)  }\CommentTok{\# but putting parenthesis around the call prints the value of \textasciigrave{}weight\_kg\textasciigrave{}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 55
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{weight\_kg          }\CommentTok{\# and so does typing the name of the object}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 55
\end{verbatim}

Now that R has \texttt{weight\_kg} in memory, we can do arithmetic with
it. For instance, we may want to convert this weight into pounds (weight
in pounds is 2.2 times the weight in kg):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FloatTok{2.2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ weight\_kg}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 121
\end{verbatim}

We can also change an object's value by assigning it a new one:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{weight\_kg }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{57.5}
\FloatTok{2.2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ weight\_kg}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 126.5
\end{verbatim}

This means that assigning a value to one object does not change the
values of other objects For example, let's store the animal's weight in
pounds in a new object, \texttt{weight\_lb}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{weight\_lb }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{2.2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ weight\_kg}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

and then change \texttt{weight\_kg} to 100.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{weight\_kg }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{100}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

R executes code in top-down order. So what happens on line 10 occurs
before line 11. What do you think is the current content of the object
\texttt{weight\_lb}? 126.5 or 220?

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Comment your code}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

The comment character in R is \texttt{\#}, anything to the right of a
\texttt{\#} in a script will be ignored by R. It is useful to leave
notes, and explanations in your scripts as demonstrated earlier.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{weight\_kg }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{57.5} \CommentTok{\# enter the weight in kg}
\NormalTok{weight\_lb }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{2.2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ weight\_kg }\CommentTok{\# convert it to lbs}
\CommentTok{\# comments can go nearly anywhere in a code chunk}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Reminder: Render your document}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Pause here and render your Quarto notes document to make sure everything
runs and displays correctly before continuing. This is a good practice
to get in to.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Functions and their
arguments}\label{functions-and-their-arguments}

Functions are ``canned scripts'' that automate more complicated sets of
commands including operations assignments, etc. Many functions are
predefined, or can be made available by importing R \emph{packages}

A function usually takes one or more inputs called \emph{arguments}, and
often (but not always) return a \emph{value}.

A typical example would be the function \texttt{sqrt()}. The input is
the number \texttt{4}, and the return value (the output) is the square
root of 4, namely 2. Executing a function is also phrased as ``calling''
the function.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 2
\end{verbatim}

Let's look into the \texttt{round} function.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(pi)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 3
\end{verbatim}

We can learn more about this function by typing \texttt{?round}. The
\textbf{Usage} section of the help documentation shows you what the
default values for each argument are. This is a very important piece to
pay attention. Sometimes the default behaviors are not what you want to
happen.

In the \textbf{Arguments} section the help file defines what each
argument does.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{x} is the object that you want to round. It must be a
  \emph{numeric vector}. In this example, \texttt{x\ =\ pi}.
\item
  \texttt{digits} is an integer indicating the number of decimal places
  to round to.
\end{itemize}

Above, we called \texttt{round()} with just one argument, \texttt{pi},
and it has returned the value \texttt{3}. That's because the default is
to round to the nearest whole number. We see that if we want a different
number of digits, we can type \texttt{digits\ =\ 2} or however many we
want.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Use the example in the help file to round the digits of pi to 2 digits.

Solution

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(pi, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 3.14
\end{verbatim}

\end{tcolorbox}

If you provide the arguments in the exact same order as they are defined
you don't have to name them:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(pi, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 3.14
\end{verbatim}

And if you do name the arguments, you can switch their order:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ pi)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 3.14
\end{verbatim}

This is a simple function with only one argument. Functions are the
backbone of how R does it's thing. You will get lots of practice with
functions, and quickly encounter functions that require many arguments.

\section{Data Types}\label{data-types}

R objects come in different data types. You can use the function
\texttt{class()} to see what data type an object is.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{class}\NormalTok{(weight\_kg)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "numeric"
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Numbers}\label{numbers}

When a number is stored in an object it is now called a
\textbf{numerical} variable. We can do math on numeric variables.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{im\_a\_number }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{50}
\FunctionTok{class}\NormalTok{(im\_a\_number)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "numeric"
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{im\_a\_number}\SpecialCharTok{*}\DecValTok{2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 100
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Letters}\label{letters}

Letters, words, and entire sentences can also be stored in objects.
These are then called \textbf{character} or \textbf{string} variables.
We can't do math on character variables, and if we try to R gives us an
error message.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(im\_a\_character }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \StringTok{"dog"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "dog"
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{class}\NormalTok{(im\_a\_character)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "character"
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{im\_a\_character}\SpecialCharTok{*}\DecValTok{2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Error in im_a_character * 2: non-numeric argument to binary operator
\end{verbatim}

In statistics classes, character variables are often treated as
\textbf{categorical} variables, which can also be called \textbf{factor}
variables. Factor variables in R are special types of categorical
variables. We will learn how to work with factor variables in week 2.

\subsection{Boolean}\label{sec-logical}

When the value of an object can only be \texttt{TRUE} or \texttt{FALSE}
it is called a \textbf{Boolean} variable. These are created by writing a
\textbf{logical statement} where the answer is either TRUE or FALSE.
Silly examples include ``Is 3 greater than 4?'' and ``Is the square root
of 4 equal to 2?''

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(huh }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{3}\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] FALSE
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{class}\NormalTok{(huh)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "logical"
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] TRUE
\end{verbatim}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Comparison Symbols}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{\textless{}} stands for ``less than''
\item
  \texttt{\textgreater{}} for ``greater than''
\item
  \texttt{\textgreater{}=} for ``greater than or equal to''
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{\textless{}=} for ``less than or equal to''
\item
  \texttt{==} for ``equal to''
\item
  \texttt{!=} for ``not equal to''
\end{itemize}

The double equal sign \texttt{==} is a test for numerical equality
between the left and right hand sides, and should not be confused with
the single \texttt{=} sign, which performs variable assignment (similar
to \texttt{\textless{}-}).

\end{tcolorbox}

We will see how to use these logical statements to do things such as
subsetting data and creating new variables in
Section~\ref{sec-cond_subset} and beyond.

\subsection{Negating Boolean values using
``!''}\label{negating-boolean-values-using}

Sometimes we want to negate a logical statement. That is, if the value
returned would be TRUE, we want to flip it to FALSE. This will be
helpful later when working with data sets to find rows that \emph{don't}
meet a certain criteria. Negation is done with an \texttt{!} exclamation
point.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DecValTok{3} \SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{4} 
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] TRUE
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\SpecialCharTok{!}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3} \SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] FALSE
\end{verbatim}

\section{Data Structures}\label{data-structures}

Data structures is how we refer to a collection of pieces of data, like
a series of numbers, or a list of words.

\subsection{Vectors}\label{vectors}

A vector is the most common and basic data structure in R, and is pretty
much the workhorse of R.

We can assign a series of values to a vector using the \texttt{c()}
function. For example we can create a vector of animal weights and
assign it to a new object \texttt{weight\_g}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(weight\_g }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{25}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{250}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{7800}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3600}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1]   25  250 7800 3600
\end{verbatim}

A vector can also contain characters:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(animals }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"mouse"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"rat"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"dog"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"cat"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "mouse" "rat"   "dog"   "cat"  
\end{verbatim}

The quotes around ``mouse'', ``rat'', etc. are essential here. Without
the quotes R will assume objects have been created called
\texttt{mouse}, \texttt{rat} and \texttt{dog}. As these objects don't
exist in R's memory, there will be an error message.

An important feature of a vector, is that all of the elements are the
same type of data. That is, each element in the vector has to be the
same type.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{class}\NormalTok{(weight\_g)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "numeric"
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{class}\NormalTok{(animals)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "character"
\end{verbatim}

If you try to mix and match data types within a vector, some
``coercion'' will occur. If you combine letters and numbers, everything
will be treated as letters.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(mix\_match }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(weight\_g, animals))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "25"    "250"   "7800"  "3600"  "mouse" "rat"   "dog"   "cat"  
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{class}\NormalTok{(mix\_match)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "character"
\end{verbatim}

This is VERY important to keep in mind when you import data into R from
another program like Excel. If you have any letters (like the word
``missing'', or ``NA'') in a column, all data from that column will be
treated as character strings. And you can't do math (such as take a
mean) on words.

Vectors are one of the many \textbf{data structures} that R uses. Other
important ones are lists (\texttt{list}), matrices (\texttt{matrix}),
data frames (\texttt{data.frame}), factors (\texttt{factor}) and arrays
(\texttt{array}). We will only talk about
\texttt{vectors},\texttt{data.frame}s and \texttt{factors} in this class
(not all in this lesson).

\subsection{Doing math on vectors}\label{doing-math-on-vectors}

You can perform math operations on the elements of a vector such as

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{weight\_KG }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ weight\_g}\SpecialCharTok{/}\DecValTok{1000}
\NormalTok{weight\_KG}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 0.025 0.250 7.800 3.600
\end{verbatim}

When adding two vectors together, the elements in the same position are
added to each other. So element 1 in the vector \texttt{a} is added to
element 1 in vector \texttt{b}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{a }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{b }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{a}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{b}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1]  7  9 11
\end{verbatim}

More complex calculations can be performed on multiple vectors.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{wt\_lb }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{155}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{135}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{90}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ht\_in }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{72}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{bmi }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{703}\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{wt\_lb }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ ht\_in}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}
\NormalTok{bmi}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 21.01948 23.17017 25.30800
\end{verbatim}

All these operations on vectors behave the same way when dealing with
variables in a data set (data.frame).

If you want to add the values \emph{within} a vector, you use functions
such as \texttt{sum()}, \texttt{max()} and \texttt{mean()}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(a)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 6
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(b)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 8
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(a}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{b)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 9
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Subsetting vectors}\label{subsetting-vectors}

If we want to extract one or several values from a vector, we must
provide one or several indices in square brackets. For instance:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{animals[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "rat"
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{animals[}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "rat" "dog"
\end{verbatim}

The number in the indices indicates which element to extract. For
example we can extract the 3rd element in \texttt{weight\_KG} by typing

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{weight\_KG[}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 7.8
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Conditional subsetting}\label{sec-cond_subset}

Another common way of subsetting is by using a logical vector.
\texttt{TRUE} will select the element with the same index, while
\texttt{FALSE} will not. Typically, these logical vectors are not typed
by hand, but are the output of other functions or logical tests such as:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{weight\_KG }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ .}\DecValTok{5}  \CommentTok{\# returns TRUE or FALSE depending on which elements that meet the condition}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] FALSE FALSE  TRUE  TRUE
\end{verbatim}

We can use this output to select elements where the value of that
logical statement is TRUE. For instance, if you wanted to select only
the values where weight in kilograms is above 1 we would type:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{weight\_KG[weight\_KG }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 7.8 3.6
\end{verbatim}

You can combine multiple tests using \texttt{\&} (both conditions are
true, AND) or \texttt{\textbar{}} (at least one of the conditions is
true, OR):

\textbf{Weight is less than 1kg or greater than 5kg}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{weight\_KG[weight\_KG }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{|}\NormalTok{ weight\_KG }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 0.025 0.250 7.800
\end{verbatim}

We can also use this technique to use the values in one vector to subset
the rows in a \emph{different} vector.

\textbf{Animals that weigh between .1 and 5kg}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{animals[weight\_KG }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=}\NormalTok{ .}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{\&}\NormalTok{ weight\_KG }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "rat" "cat"
\end{verbatim}

A common task is to search for certain strings in a vector. One could
use the ``or'' operator \texttt{\textbar{}} to test for equality to
multiple values, but this can quickly become tedious. The function
\texttt{\%in\%} allows you to test if any of the elements of a search
vector are found:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{animals[animals }\SpecialCharTok{==} \StringTok{"cat"} \SpecialCharTok{|}\NormalTok{ animals }\SpecialCharTok{==} \StringTok{"rat"}\NormalTok{] }\CommentTok{\# returns both rat and cat}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "rat" "cat"
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{animals }\SpecialCharTok{\%in\%} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"rat"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"cat"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"dog"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"duck"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"goat"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] FALSE  TRUE  TRUE  TRUE
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
