(linear model) line in blue, and the loess line (by not specifying a
method) in red.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{flipper\_length\_mm, }\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{body\_mass\_g)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{se=}\ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{method=}\StringTok{"lm"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{color=}\StringTok{"red"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{se=}\ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{color=}\StringTok{"blue"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-17-1.pdf}}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ggpubr}}{ggpubr}}

You can add \emph{either} a linear model line \emph{or} a loess line to
a \texttt{ggscatter} using the \texttt{add=} argument.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggscatter}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{x=}\StringTok{"flipper\_length\_mm"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{y=}\StringTok{"body\_mass\_g"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{add =} \StringTok{"loess"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{ggscatter}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{x=}\StringTok{"flipper\_length\_mm"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{y=}\StringTok{"body\_mass\_g"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{add =} \StringTok{"reg.line"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-18-1.pdf}}\end{minipage}%
%
\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-18-2.pdf}}\end{minipage}%

\end{figure}%

\section{One continuous vs.~one
categorical}\label{one-continuous-vs.-one-categorical}

The tactic here is to create an appropriate plot for a continuous
variable, and then \texttt{fill} the geometric area or \texttt{color}
the lines depending on the level of the categorical variable.

\subsection{Histograms}\label{histograms}

Neither \texttt{fill}ing or \texttt{color}ing the histogram bars using
\texttt{ggplot2} depending on the group work well due to the overlap.

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ggplot2}}{ggplot2}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{body\_mass\_g, }
                   \AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{species)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_histogram}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-19-1.pdf}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{body\_mass\_g, }
                   \AttributeTok{color=}\NormalTok{species)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_histogram}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-20-1.pdf}}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ggpubr}}{ggpubr}}

The defaults for \texttt{gghistogram} automatically adjusts the
transparency of the histogram bars to make the overlap a little less
troublesome, but it doesn't always work well.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{gghistogram}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"body\_mass\_g"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"species"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-21-1.pdf}}

\subsection{Density curves}\label{density-curves-1}

Similar to histograms, you can \texttt{fill} or \texttt{color} the
density curves depending on the group.

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ggplot-fill}}{ggplot-fill}}

It's still hard to see some groups due to the overlap, so we adjust the
transparency by applying a value to \texttt{alpha} inside the
\texttt{geom\_density} layer. Alpha is a measure of transparency, from
0=clear to 1=opaque.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{body\_mass\_g, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{species)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_density}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{alpha=}\NormalTok{.}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-22-1.pdf}}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ggplot-color}}{ggplot-color}}

You could also just color the lines and leave the fill alone.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{body\_mass\_g, }\AttributeTok{color=}\NormalTok{species)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_density}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-23-1.pdf}}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ggpubr}}{ggpubr}}

The \texttt{ggdensity} function also has \texttt{color} and
\texttt{fill} options, where the transparency of the density plots are
automatically handled.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggdensity}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{x=}\StringTok{"body\_mass\_g"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"species"}\NormalTok{) }\CommentTok{\# left}
\FunctionTok{ggdensity}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{x=}\StringTok{"body\_mass\_g"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"species"}\NormalTok{) }\CommentTok{\# right}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-24-1.pdf}}\end{minipage}%
%
\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-24-2.pdf}}\end{minipage}%

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Boxplots}\label{boxplots-1}

To create grouped boxplots, put the continuous variable on one axis, and
the categorical on the other axis.

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ggplot2}}{ggplot2}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{body\_mass\_g, }\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{species)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{() }\CommentTok{\# left}
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{species, }\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{body\_mass\_g)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{() }\CommentTok{\# right}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-25-1.pdf}}\end{minipage}%
%
\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-25-2.pdf}}\end{minipage}%

\end{figure}%

If you want an additional color feature (and the corresponding legend),
you can either \texttt{fill} or \texttt{color} the boxes by the same
categorical variable.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{body\_mass\_g, }\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{species, }\AttributeTok{fill =}\NormalTok{ species)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{() }\CommentTok{\# left}
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{species, }\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{body\_mass\_g, }\AttributeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ species)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{() }\CommentTok{\# right}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-26-1.pdf}}\end{minipage}%
%
\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-26-2.pdf}}\end{minipage}%

\end{figure}%

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ggpubr}}{ggpubr}}

Not much difference in the style between the \texttt{ggplot2} and
\texttt{ggboxplot} versions. This method uses \emph{slightly} less code.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggboxplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{y=}\StringTok{"body\_mass\_g"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"species"}\NormalTok{) }\CommentTok{\# left}
\FunctionTok{ggboxplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{y=}\StringTok{"body\_mass\_g"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"species"}\NormalTok{) }\CommentTok{\# right}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-27-1.pdf}}\end{minipage}%
%
\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-27-2.pdf}}\end{minipage}%

\end{figure}%

\section{Two categorical variables}\label{two-categorical-variables}

Recall from Section Section~\ref{sec-intro-tables} that frequency tables
are a common way to summarize categorical variables, and that both the
frequency and relative percent are important summary numbers. Those
percentages are even more important when comparing the joint
distribution of two categorical variables.

Cross-tabs, cross-tabulations and two-way tables are different names for
the same thing, and can be created by using the \texttt{table()} and
\texttt{tbl\_summary} functions. The values in each cell are the number
of observations in that combination of characteristics.
{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}We use this tactic in Chapter
Chapter~\ref{sec-dm} to check our recodes.\end{footnotesize}}}

Let's explore the relationship between the penguins sex and species.

\subsection{Frequency and proportion
tables}\label{frequency-and-proportion-tables}

\section{base R}

The first argument \texttt{species} specifies the levels that show on
the rows, the second argument \texttt{sex} specifies the columns.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{species, pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sex)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
           
            female male
  Adelie        73   73
  Chinstrap     34   34
  Gentoo        58   61
\end{verbatim}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{tbl\_summary}}{tbl\_summary}}

To achieve the same ordering with species on the rows and sex on the
columns, we \texttt{include="species"} and set \texttt{by\ =\ "sex"}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{tbl\_summary}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{include =} \StringTok{"species"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{by =} \StringTok{"sex"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.4pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}lcc}
\toprule
\textbf{Characteristic} & \textbf{female}  N = 165\textsuperscript{\textit{1}} & \textbf{male}  N = 168\textsuperscript{\textit{1}} \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
species &  &  \\ 
    Adelie & 73 (44\%) & 73 (43\%) \\ 
    Chinstrap & 34 (21\%) & 34 (20\%) \\ 
    Gentoo & 58 (35\%) & 61 (36\%) \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\begin{minipage}{\linewidth}
\textsuperscript{\textit{1}}n (\%)\\
\end{minipage}
\end{table}

There are 73 female Adelie penguins, and 61 male Gentoo penguins.

\subsubsection{Proportions}\label{proportions}

By default, when we ask for proportions we get the \textbf{cell}
proportions. That is, the percent out of \emph{all} penguins in that
data set that have that combination of traits. The percents add up to 1
across the entire table.

\section{base R}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{species, pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sex) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{prop.table}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
           
               female      male
  Adelie    0.2192192 0.2192192
  Chinstrap 0.1021021 0.1021021
  Gentoo    0.1741742 0.1831832
\end{verbatim}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{tbl\_summary}}{tbl\_summary}}

Note that \texttt{gtsummary} tables tend to round pretty heavily.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{tbl\_summary}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{include =} \StringTok{"species"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{by =} \StringTok{"sex"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{percent =} \StringTok{"cell"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.4pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}lcc}
\toprule
\textbf{Characteristic} & \textbf{female}  N = 165\textsuperscript{\textit{1}} & \textbf{male}  N = 168\textsuperscript{\textit{1}} \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
species &  &  \\ 
    Adelie & 73 (22\%) & 73 (22\%) \\ 
    Chinstrap & 34 (10\%) & 34 (10\%) \\ 
    Gentoo & 58 (17\%) & 61 (18\%) \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\begin{minipage}{\linewidth}
\textsuperscript{\textit{1}}n (\%)\\
\end{minipage}
\end{table}

21.9\% of all penguins are Adelie females, 18.3\% of all penguins are
male Gentoo's.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Comparing percentages}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

More often than not, we want to compare percents of one group -- within
each level of the other group. For example is the male to female ratio
the same for each species?

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsubsection{Row percents}\label{sec-row-pct-table}

To compare the distribution of sex (columns) within each of the sepecies
(rows) we need row percentages. The percentages now add up to 1 across
the rows and the comparison groups are each species.

\section{base R}

Specify \texttt{margin=1} inside the \texttt{prop.table()}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{species, pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sex) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{prop.table}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{margin=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)  }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
           
            female  male
  Adelie     0.500 0.500
  Chinstrap  0.500 0.500
  Gentoo     0.487 0.513
\end{verbatim}

I added a \texttt{round} function to the end of this because noone needs
that many decimal places.

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{tbl\_summary}}{tbl\_summary}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{tbl\_summary}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{include =} \StringTok{"species"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{by =} \StringTok{"sex"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{percent =} \StringTok{"row"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.4pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}lcc}
\toprule
\textbf{Characteristic} & \textbf{female}  N = 165\textsuperscript{\textit{1}} & \textbf{male}  N = 168\textsuperscript{\textit{1}} \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
species &  &  \\ 
    Adelie & 73 (50\%) & 73 (50\%) \\ 
    Chinstrap & 34 (50\%) & 34 (50\%) \\ 
    Gentoo & 58 (49\%) & 61 (51\%) \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\begin{minipage}{\linewidth}
\textsuperscript{\textit{1}}n (\%)\\
\end{minipage}
\end{table}

50\% \emph{of Adelie penguins} are male, but 51.3\% \emph{of Gentoo
penguins} are male.

\subsubsection{Column percents}\label{column-percents}

To compare the distribution of species (rows) within each of the columns
(sex) we need column percentages. The percentages now add up to 1 down
the columns and the comparison groups are male and female.

\section{base R}

Specify \texttt{margin=2} in \texttt{prop.table()}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{species, pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sex) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{prop.table}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{margin=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
           
            female  male
  Adelie     0.442 0.435
  Chinstrap  0.206 0.202
  Gentoo     0.352 0.363
\end{verbatim}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{tbl\_summary}}{tbl\_summary}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{tbl\_summary}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{include =} \StringTok{"species"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{by =} \StringTok{"sex"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{percent =} \StringTok{"column"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.4pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}lcc}
\toprule
\textbf{Characteristic} & \textbf{female}  N = 165\textsuperscript{\textit{1}} & \textbf{male}  N = 168\textsuperscript{\textit{1}} \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
species &  &  \\ 
    Adelie & 73 (44\%) & 73 (43\%) \\ 
    Chinstrap & 34 (21\%) & 34 (20\%) \\ 
    Gentoo & 58 (35\%) & 61 (36\%) \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\begin{minipage}{\linewidth}
\textsuperscript{\textit{1}}n (\%)\\
\end{minipage}
\end{table}

44\% \emph{of female penguins} are Adelie species, 36\% \emph{of male
penguins} are Gentoo.

\subsection{Stacked bar charts}\label{stacked-bar-charts}

Sometimes pictures are better than tables, so let's try to apply the
same \texttt{fill} tactic that we used in the last section.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{species, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{sex)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-36-1.pdf}}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Stacked barcharts are the ggplot2 default}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Stacked barcharts are generally only useful when comparing percents out
of a whole. To get the correct view you can add a
\texttt{position\ =\ "fill"} argument to the \texttt{geom\_bar()} layer.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{species, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{sex)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{position =} \StringTok{"fill"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{ylab}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Proportion"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-37-1.pdf}}

\subsection{Side by side bar charts}\label{side-by-side-bar-charts}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ggplot2}}{ggplot2}}

Add the argument \texttt{position=dodge} inside the \texttt{geom\_bar}
layer to put the bars side by side.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{species, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{sex)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{position =} \StringTok{"dodge"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-38-1.pdf}}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{sjPlot}}{sjPlot}}

The \texttt{plot\_xtab} function is the two-way table analogy to
\texttt{plot\_frq} to create a barchart with clear lables on the bars
for the N and \% (and NA values dropped). Note you have to use dollar
sign notation here for the variables.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{plot\_xtab}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{species, }\AttributeTok{grp =}\NormalTok{ pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sex)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-39-1.pdf}}

By default this plots the vertical axis as percents, not counts, and it
shows the marginal total for the variable that's on the x-axis. We can
remove the total by setting \texttt{show.total} to \texttt{false}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{plot\_xtab}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{species, }\AttributeTok{grp =}\NormalTok{ pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sex, }
          \AttributeTok{show.total =} \StringTok{"false"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-40-1.pdf}}

As before, typically we aren't interested in comparing proportions out
of the whole, but proportions out of one of the two margins (variables).

\subsection{Comparing Percents}\label{comparing-percents}

Our eyes make comparisons the best when the bars are physically close to
each other. So generally you want to put the groups we want to compare
\emph{within} on the x-axis, and have separate bars for each level of
the variable we want to compare \emph{across}.

See each tab on how to compare the distribution of \texttt{sex} within
each \texttt{species}. This corresponds to the \textbf{row} percents
from Section~\ref{sec-row-pct-table} .

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ggplot2}}{ggplot2}}

We have to pre-aggregate the data to calculate the grouped percents
before we create the plot. Notice how this changes the variable names.
Put \texttt{species} (\texttt{Var1}) on the x-axis, \texttt{fill} by
\texttt{sex} (\texttt{Var2}), and \texttt{weight} the \texttt{Freq}
variable. {\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}I also changed the y axis
label here to make it more clear that the heights of the bars are a
percent\end{footnotesize}}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(pen.table }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{species, pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sex) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{as.data.frame}\NormalTok{()) }\CommentTok{\# Printing to show the variable names have changed}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
       Var1   Var2 Freq
1    Adelie female   73
2 Chinstrap female   34
3    Gentoo female   58
4    Adelie   male   73
5 Chinstrap   male   34
6    Gentoo   male   61
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen.table, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{Var1, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{Var2, }\AttributeTok{weight =}\NormalTok{ Freq)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{position =} \StringTok{"dodge"}\NormalTok{)  }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{ylab}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Proportion"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-41-1.pdf}}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{sjPlot}}{sjPlot}}

Put \texttt{species} on the x-axis and \texttt{grp} by \texttt{sex}, and
specify \texttt{margin\ =\ "row"}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{plot\_xtab}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{species, }\AttributeTok{grp =}\NormalTok{ pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sex, }\AttributeTok{show.total =} \StringTok{"false"}\NormalTok{, }
          \AttributeTok{margin =} \StringTok{"row"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-42-1.pdf}}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Your Turn}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Modify the code above to create a plot to compare the distribution of
species within each sex.

ggplot2 solution

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen.table, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{Var2, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{Var1, }\AttributeTok{weight =}\NormalTok{ Freq)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{position =} \StringTok{"dodge"}\NormalTok{)  }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{ylab}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Proportion"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-43-1.pdf}}

sjPlot solution

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{plot\_xtab}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sex, }\AttributeTok{grp =}\NormalTok{ pen}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{species, }\AttributeTok{show.total =} \StringTok{"false"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{margin =} \StringTok{"row"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots1_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-44-1.pdf}}

\end{tcolorbox}

\chapter{Efficient data management}\label{sec-dplyr}

When working with data you must:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Figure out what you want to do.
\item
  Precisely describe what you want in the form of a computer program.
\item
  Execute the code.
\end{enumerate}

The dplyr package makes each of these steps as fast and easy as possible
by:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Elucidating the most common data manipulation operations, so that your
  options are helpfully constrained when thinking about how to tackle a
  problem.
\item
  Providing simple functions that correspond to the most common data
  manipulation verbs, so that you can easily translate your thoughts
  into code.
\item
  Using efficient data storage back ends, so that you spend as little
  time waiting for the computer as possible.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={🎓 Learning Objectives}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

After completing this lesson students will be able to

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Explain the difference between a \texttt{data.table} and a
  \texttt{tibble}
\item
  Build and execute a chain of command to accomplish a data management
  task
\item
  Extract certain rows using \texttt{select}
\item
  Reorder columns using \texttt{relocate}, sort using \texttt{arrange},
  and rename using \texttt{rename}
\item
  Create new variables using \texttt{mutate} and \texttt{case\_when}
\item
  Subset rows based on a logical criteria using \texttt{filter}
\item
  Create summary statistics using \texttt{group\_by} and
  \texttt{summarize}
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Prepare}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Open your Math 130 R Project.
\item
  Right click and ``save as'' this lessons
  \href{notes/dplyr_notes.qmd}{{[}Quarto notes file{]}} and save into
  your \texttt{Math130/notes} folder.
\item
  In the \emph{Files} pane, open this Quarto file and Render this file.
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}
\NormalTok{flights }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ nycflights13}\SpecialCharTok{::}\NormalTok{flights}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{nycflights13} package contains several data sets that can be
used to help understand what causes delays. We will be using the
\texttt{flights} data set which contains information about all flights
that departed from NYC (e.g.~EWR, JFK and LGA) in 2013.

\section{Tibbles}\label{sec-tibble}

The \texttt{flights} data set, and any data set created with
\texttt{dplyr}, has a specific data type called a \texttt{tibble}. These
are not as furry and prolific as their cousins the \texttt{tribbles}.
\texttt{tibbles} behaves for all intents and purposes as a
\texttt{data.frame}, just gets displayed differently. For example, the
\texttt{flights} data set contains data on 19 characteristics
(variables) from 336,776 flights. There's no way I would want to print
out a data set that large. But I'm gonna\ldots.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{flights}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 336,776 x 19
    year month   day dep_time sched_dep_time dep_delay arr_time sched_arr_time
   <int> <int> <int>    <int>          <int>     <dbl>    <int>          <int>
 1  2013     1     1      517            515         2      830            819
 2  2013     1     1      533            529         4      850            830
 3  2013     1     1      542            540         2      923            850
 4  2013     1     1      544            545        -1     1004           1022
 5  2013     1     1      554            600        -6      812            837
 6  2013     1     1      554            558        -4      740            728
 7  2013     1     1      555            600        -5      913            854
 8  2013     1     1      557            600        -3      709            723
 9  2013     1     1      557            600        -3      838            846
10  2013     1     1      558            600        -2      753            745
# i 336,766 more rows
# i 11 more variables: arr_delay <dbl>, carrier <chr>, flight <int>,
#   tailnum <chr>, origin <chr>, dest <chr>, air_time <dbl>, distance <dbl>,
#   hour <dbl>, minute <dbl>, time_hour <dttm>
\end{verbatim}

The output has been trimmed to something more reasonable for our viewing
pleasure. This may not seem such a big deal because R Studio already
provides some level of truncation for our viewing pleasure, but when you
accidentally print out 400 pages of data set in your homework
assignment, your instructor (and your wallet if you print it without
looking) won't be happy.

\section{Basic verbs}\label{basic-verbs}

The \texttt{dplyr} package contains many functions to perform data
manipulation ``actions'', also called verbs. We will look at the
following verbs:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{filter()}: Returns a subset of the rows
\item
  \texttt{select()}: Returns only the listed columns
\item
  \texttt{rename()}: Renames the variables listed
\item
  \texttt{mutate()}: Adds columns from existing data
\item
  \texttt{relocate()}: Moves the order of the columns
\item
  \texttt{arrange()}: Sorts the rows by the value of a variable
\item
  \texttt{case\_when()}: A useful expansion of \texttt{ifelse}
\item
  \texttt{summarise()}: Reduces each group to a single row by
  calculating aggregate measures
\item
  \texttt{group\_by()}: Groups a data set on a factor variable, such
  that all functions performed are then done on each level of the factor
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Filter}\label{filter}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/filter.png}}

}

\caption{Cartoon showing three fuzzy monsters either selecting or
crossing out rows of a data table. If the type of animal in the table is
``otter'' and the site is ``bay'', a monster is drawing a purple
rectangle around the row. If those conditions are not met, another
monster is putting a line through the column indicating it will be
excluded. Stylized text reads ``dplyr::filter() - keep rows that satisfy
your conditions.''}

\end{figure}%

{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}Learn more about
\href{https://dplyr.tidyverse.org/reference/filter.html}{dplyr::filter}.\end{footnotesize}}}

\texttt{filter()} allows you to select a subset of the rows of a data
frame. The first argument is the name of the data frame, and the second
and subsequent are filtering expressions evaluated in the context of
that data frame. For example, we can select all flights on January 1st
with

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(flights, month }\SpecialCharTok{==} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, day }\SpecialCharTok{==} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 842 x 19
    year month   day dep_time sched_dep_time dep_delay arr_time sched_arr_time
   <int> <int> <int>    <int>          <int>     <dbl>    <int>          <int>
 1  2013     1     1      517            515         2      830            819
 2  2013     1     1      533            529         4      850            830
 3  2013     1     1      542            540         2      923            850
 4  2013     1     1      544            545        -1     1004           1022
 5  2013     1     1      554            600        -6      812            837
 6  2013     1     1      554            558        -4      740            728
 7  2013     1     1      555            600        -5      913            854
 8  2013     1     1      557            600        -3      709            723
 9  2013     1     1      557            600        -3      838            846
10  2013     1     1      558            600        -2      753            745
# i 832 more rows
# i 11 more variables: arr_delay <dbl>, carrier <chr>, flight <int>,
#   tailnum <chr>, origin <chr>, dest <chr>, air_time <dbl>, distance <dbl>,
#   hour <dbl>, minute <dbl>, time_hour <dttm>
\end{verbatim}

\texttt{filter()} works similarly to \texttt{subset()} except that you
can give it any number of filtering conditions which are joined together
with \&. You can use other Boolean operators explicitly. Here we select
flights in January or February.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(flights, month }\SpecialCharTok{==} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{|}\NormalTok{ month }\SpecialCharTok{==} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 51,955 x 19
    year month   day dep_time sched_dep_time dep_delay arr_time sched_arr_time
   <int> <int> <int>    <int>          <int>     <dbl>    <int>          <int>
 1  2013     1     1      517            515         2      830            819
 2  2013     1     1      533            529         4      850            830
 3  2013     1     1      542            540         2      923            850
 4  2013     1     1      544            545        -1     1004           1022
 5  2013     1     1      554            600        -6      812            837
 6  2013     1     1      554            558        -4      740            728
 7  2013     1     1      555            600        -5      913            854
 8  2013     1     1      557            600        -3      709            723
 9  2013     1     1      557            600        -3      838            846
10  2013     1     1      558            600        -2      753            745
# i 51,945 more rows
# i 11 more variables: arr_delay <dbl>, carrier <chr>, flight <int>,
#   tailnum <chr>, origin <chr>, dest <chr>, air_time <dbl>, distance <dbl>,
#   hour <dbl>, minute <dbl>, time_hour <dttm>
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Select}\label{select}

Often you work with large data sets with many columns where only a few
are actually of interest to you. \texttt{select()} allows you to rapidly
zoom in on a useful subset using operations that usually only work on
numeric variable positions.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(flights, month, day, year)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 336,776 x 3
   month   day  year
   <int> <int> <int>
 1     1     1  2013
 2     1     1  2013
 3     1     1  2013
 4     1     1  2013
 5     1     1  2013
 6     1     1  2013
 7     1     1  2013
 8     1     1  2013
 9     1     1  2013
10     1     1  2013
# i 336,766 more rows
\end{verbatim}

You can use a colon (:) to select all columns physically located between
two variables.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(flights, year}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{day)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 336,776 x 3
    year month   day
   <int> <int> <int>
 1  2013     1     1
 2  2013     1     1
 3  2013     1     1
 4  2013     1     1
 5  2013     1     1
 6  2013     1     1
 7  2013     1     1
 8  2013     1     1
 9  2013     1     1
10  2013     1     1
# i 336,766 more rows
\end{verbatim}

To exclude specific columns you use the minus sign (-)

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(flights, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{carrier)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 336,776 x 18
    year month   day dep_time sched_dep_time dep_delay arr_time sched_arr_time
   <int> <int> <int>    <int>          <int>     <dbl>    <int>          <int>
 1  2013     1     1      517            515         2      830            819
 2  2013     1     1      533            529         4      850            830
 3  2013     1     1      542            540         2      923            850
 4  2013     1     1      544            545        -1     1004           1022
 5  2013     1     1      554            600        -6      812            837
 6  2013     1     1      554            558        -4      740            728
 7  2013     1     1      555            600        -5      913            854
 8  2013     1     1      557            600        -3      709            723
 9  2013     1     1      557            600        -3      838            846
10  2013     1     1      558            600        -2      753            745
# i 336,766 more rows
# i 10 more variables: arr_delay <dbl>, flight <int>, tailnum <chr>,
#   origin <chr>, dest <chr>, air_time <dbl>, distance <dbl>, hour <dbl>,
#   minute <dbl>, time_hour <dttm>
\end{verbatim}

This also works to exclude all columns EXCEPT the ones between two
variables.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(flights, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{(year}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{day))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 336,776 x 16
   dep_time sched_dep_time dep_delay arr_time sched_arr_time arr_delay carrier
      <int>          <int>     <dbl>    <int>          <int>     <dbl> <chr>  
 1      517            515         2      830            819        11 UA     
 2      533            529         4      850            830        20 UA     
 3      542            540         2      923            850        33 AA     
 4      544            545        -1     1004           1022       -18 B6     
 5      554            600        -6      812            837       -25 DL     
 6      554            558        -4      740            728        12 UA     
 7      555            600        -5      913            854        19 B6     
 8      557            600        -3      709            723       -14 EV     
 9      557            600        -3      838            846        -8 B6     
10      558            600        -2      753            745         8 AA     
# i 336,766 more rows
# i 9 more variables: flight <int>, tailnum <chr>, origin <chr>, dest <chr>,
#   air_time <dbl>, distance <dbl>, hour <dbl>, minute <dbl>, time_hour <dttm>
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Rename}\label{rename}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/rename.png}}

}

\caption{Header text: ``dplyr:: rename() - rename columns''. Below, an
illustration of a mouse standing on a wedge of swiss cheese, changing
column names from ``species'' to ``nemesis,'' and ``site'' to ``lair.''
On a cork board next to the mouse are images of ``Cheese thieves''
suspects (narwhal, chicken and pika) and maps of their locations.}

\end{figure}%

{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}Learn more about
\href{https://dplyr.tidyverse.org/reference/rename.html}{dplyr::rename}\end{footnotesize}}}

Sometimes variables come to you in really obscure naming conventions.
What the heck is SBA641? The \texttt{rename()} function converts an
\texttt{old\ name} to a \texttt{new\ name}. The generic syntax is
\texttt{rename(new\ =\ old)}. {\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}For the
purpose of these lecture notes I am not making this change permenant.
There is no assignment operator \texttt{\textless{}-} used here, so this
change is not going to persist into later code.\end{footnotesize}}}

So to rename \texttt{dep\_time} to \texttt{departure\_time} we would
type

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{rename}\NormalTok{(flights, }\AttributeTok{departure\_time =}\NormalTok{ dep\_time)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 336,776 x 19
    year month   day departure_time sched_dep_time dep_delay arr_time
   <int> <int> <int>          <int>          <int>     <dbl>    <int>
 1  2013     1     1            517            515         2      830
 2  2013     1     1            533            529         4      850
 3  2013     1     1            542            540         2      923
 4  2013     1     1            544            545        -1     1004
 5  2013     1     1            554            600        -6      812
 6  2013     1     1            554            558        -4      740
 7  2013     1     1            555            600        -5      913
 8  2013     1     1            557            600        -3      709
 9  2013     1     1            557            600        -3      838
10  2013     1     1            558            600        -2      753
# i 336,766 more rows
# i 12 more variables: sched_arr_time <int>, arr_delay <dbl>, carrier <chr>,
#   flight <int>, tailnum <chr>, origin <chr>, dest <chr>, air_time <dbl>,
#   distance <dbl>, hour <dbl>, minute <dbl>, time_hour <dttm>
\end{verbatim}

The variable name on the 3rd column now says \texttt{departure\_time}
instead of \texttt{dep\_time}.

\subsection{Mutate}\label{mutate}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/mutate.png}}

}

\caption{Cartoon of cute fuzzy monsters dressed up as different X-men
characters, working together to add a new column to an existing}

\end{figure}%

{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}Learn more about
\href{https://dplyr.tidyverse.org/reference/mutate.html}{dplyr::mutate}\end{footnotesize}}}

As well as selecting from the set of existing columns, it's often useful
to add new columns that are functions of existing columns. This is the
job of \texttt{mutate()}!

Here we create two variables: gain (as arrival delay minus departure
delay) and speed (as distance divided by time, converted to hours).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{a }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(flights, }\AttributeTok{gain =}\NormalTok{ arr\_delay }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ dep\_delay, }
                \AttributeTok{speed =}\NormalTok{ distance }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ air\_time }\SpecialCharTok{*} \DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(a, gain, distance, air\_time, speed)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 336,776 x 4
    gain distance air_time speed
   <dbl>    <dbl>    <dbl> <dbl>
 1     9     1400      227  370.
 2    16     1416      227  374.
 3    31     1089      160  408.
 4   -17     1576      183  517.
 5   -19      762      116  394.
 6    16      719      150  288.
 7    24     1065      158  404.
 8   -11      229       53  259.
 9    -5      944      140  405.
10    10      733      138  319.
# i 336,766 more rows
\end{verbatim}

One key advantage of mutate is that you can refer to the columns you
just created. In one mutate call we can create the \texttt{gain}
variable, and then use it to create \texttt{gain\_per\_hour}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(flights, }\AttributeTok{gain =}\NormalTok{ arr\_delay }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ dep\_delay, }
                \AttributeTok{gain\_per\_hour =}\NormalTok{ gain }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (air\_time }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{ ))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 336,776 x 21
    year month   day dep_time sched_dep_time dep_delay arr_time sched_arr_time
   <int> <int> <int>    <int>          <int>     <dbl>    <int>          <int>
 1  2013     1     1      517            515         2      830            819
 2  2013     1     1      533            529         4      850            830
 3  2013     1     1      542            540         2      923            850
 4  2013     1     1      544            545        -1     1004           1022
 5  2013     1     1      554            600        -6      812            837
 6  2013     1     1      554            558        -4      740            728
 7  2013     1     1      555            600        -5      913            854
 8  2013     1     1      557            600        -3      709            723
 9  2013     1     1      557            600        -3      838            846
10  2013     1     1      558            600        -2      753            745
# i 336,766 more rows
# i 13 more variables: arr_delay <dbl>, carrier <chr>, flight <int>,
#   tailnum <chr>, origin <chr>, dest <chr>, air_time <dbl>, distance <dbl>,
#   hour <dbl>, minute <dbl>, time_hour <dttm>, gain <dbl>, gain_per_hour <dbl>
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Summarize}\label{summarize}

The last verb is \texttt{summarise()}, which is most often used to
calculate summary statistics.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(flights, }
          \AttributeTok{ave\_departure\_delay =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(dep\_delay, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{), }
          \AttributeTok{n\_flights\_with\_delay =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(dep\_delay)}\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{), }
          \AttributeTok{max\_depart\_delay =} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(dep\_delay, }\AttributeTok{na.rm=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 1 x 3
  ave_departure_delay n_flights_with_delay max_depart_delay
                <dbl>                <int>            <dbl>
1                12.6               328521             1301
\end{verbatim}

It may seem like a lot of code compared to base R functions such as
\texttt{mean()}, \texttt{sum()} and \texttt{max()}, but we'll see how
this is used in combination with grouping in the next section to easily
create grouped summary statistics.

\subsection{Arrange}\label{arrange}

The \texttt{arrange()} function changes the order of the rows for the
whole data set based on the value of the variable being sorted.

Lets use a smaller data set with fewer variables so it's easier to
visualize.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{flights2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(flights, distance, origin, dep\_delay)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{Sort by one variable}\label{sort-by-one-variable}

Sort flights based on distance, from shortest to longest. This is
Ascending order.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{arrange}\NormalTok{(flights2, distance)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 336,776 x 3
   distance origin dep_delay
      <dbl> <chr>      <dbl>
 1       17 EWR           NA
 2       80 EWR           -2
 3       80 EWR           40
 4       80 EWR          134
 5       80 EWR           -1
 6       80 EWR           -5
 7       80 EWR           -4
 8       80 EWR           -5
 9       80 EWR           -3
10       80 EWR           -3
# i 336,766 more rows
\end{verbatim}

To reverse the sort order, use \texttt{desc()}. This is Decending order.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{arrange}\NormalTok{(flights2, }\FunctionTok{desc}\NormalTok{(distance))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 336,776 x 3
   distance origin dep_delay
      <dbl> <chr>      <dbl>
 1     4983 JFK           -3
 2     4983 JFK            9
 3     4983 JFK           14
 4     4983 JFK            0
 5     4983 JFK           -2
 6     4983 JFK           79
 7     4983 JFK          102
 8     4983 JFK            1
 9     4983 JFK         1301
10     4983 JFK           -1
# i 336,766 more rows
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Sort by two variables}\label{sort-by-two-variables}

You can sort by more than one variable. Rows are ordered by the first
variable, and ties are broken using the second variable.

Here we sort flights first by origin airport, then by departure delay
within each airport.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{arrange}\NormalTok{(flights2, origin, dep\_delay)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 336,776 x 3
   distance origin dep_delay
      <dbl> <chr>      <dbl>
 1      631 EWR          -25
 2      200 EWR          -23
 3      200 EWR          -23
 4      200 EWR          -22
 5      266 EWR          -22
 6     2402 EWR          -21
 7      200 EWR          -21
 8      200 EWR          -21
 9      937 EWR          -20
10     1065 EWR          -20
# i 336,766 more rows
\end{verbatim}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Avoid the base \texttt{sort} function}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

There is a \texttt{sort} and \texttt{order} functions in base R, but
they operate on a single vector only. They do \emph{not} keep all rows
in a data frame together when sorting rows, so avoid using it.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Relocate}\label{relocate}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/relocate.png}}

}

\caption{Cartoon of fuzzy monsters moving columns around in fork lifts,
while one supervises. Stylized text reads ``dplyr::relocate() - move
columns around! Default: move to FRONT , or move to .before or .after a
specified column.''}

\end{figure}%

{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}Learn more about
\href{https://dplyr.tidyverse.org/reference/relocate.html}{dplyr::relocate}\end{footnotesize}}}

Use \texttt{relocate()} to change column positions, using the same
syntax as select() to make it easy to move blocks of columns at once.

\textbf{Current order of names} for reference purposes.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{names}\NormalTok{(flights)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
 [1] "year"           "month"          "day"            "dep_time"      
 [5] "sched_dep_time" "dep_delay"      "arr_time"       "sched_arr_time"
 [9] "arr_delay"      "carrier"        "flight"         "tailnum"       
[13] "origin"         "dest"           "air_time"       "distance"      
[17] "hour"           "minute"         "time_hour"     
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Bring \texttt{origin} to the front}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{flights }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ flights }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} 
  \FunctionTok{relocate}\NormalTok{(origin)}
\FunctionTok{names}\NormalTok{(flights)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
 [1] "origin"         "year"           "month"          "day"           
 [5] "dep_time"       "sched_dep_time" "dep_delay"      "arr_time"      
 [9] "sched_arr_time" "arr_delay"      "carrier"        "flight"        
[13] "tailnum"        "dest"           "air_time"       "distance"      
[17] "hour"           "minute"         "time_hour"     
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Move \texttt{dest} right after \texttt{origin}} using the
\texttt{.after} argument.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{flights }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ flights }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} 
  \FunctionTok{relocate}\NormalTok{(dest, }\AttributeTok{.after =}\NormalTok{ origin)}
\FunctionTok{names}\NormalTok{(flights)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
 [1] "origin"         "dest"           "year"           "month"         
 [5] "day"            "dep_time"       "sched_dep_time" "dep_delay"     
 [9] "arr_time"       "sched_arr_time" "arr_delay"      "carrier"       
[13] "flight"         "tailnum"        "air_time"       "distance"      
[17] "hour"           "minute"         "time_hour"     
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Move multiple variables to the end}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{flights }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ flights }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} 
  \FunctionTok{relocate}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(carrier, flight, tailnum), }\AttributeTok{.after =} \FunctionTok{last\_col}\NormalTok{())}
\FunctionTok{names}\NormalTok{(flights)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
 [1] "origin"         "dest"           "year"           "month"         
 [5] "day"            "dep_time"       "sched_dep_time" "dep_delay"     
 [9] "arr_time"       "sched_arr_time" "arr_delay"      "air_time"      
[13] "distance"       "hour"           "minute"         "time_hour"     
[17] "carrier"        "flight"         "tailnum"       
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Case when}\label{case-when}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/case_when.png}}

}

\caption{Cartoon showing a table with creature type (kraken, dragon, or
cyclops) and age (baby, teen, or adult). The three creatures listed are
adding a new column named ``danger'', which contains the word
``extreme!'' if the type is ``kraken'', or ``high'' for any other type.
Stylized text reads ``dplyr::case\_when() - IF ELSE\ldots but you love
it? An example of code is shown: mutate(danger = case\_when(type ==
``kraken'' \textasciitilde{} ``extreme!'', TRUE \textasciitilde{}
``high'').}

\end{figure}%

{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}Learn more about
\href{https://dplyr.tidyverse.org/reference/case_when.html}{case\_when}\end{footnotesize}}}

This function allows you to vectorise multiple \texttt{if\_else()}
statements. Each case is evaluated sequentially and the first match for
each element determines the corresponding value in the output vector. If
no cases match, the \texttt{.default} is used as a final ``else''
statment.

The general syntax is \texttt{logical\ statment} \textasciitilde{}
\texttt{value}. On the left hand side you specify a logical statement to
identify a certain ``case'', then a tilde \texttt{\textasciitilde{}},
then on the right hand side you specify what you want the value of the
new variable to be when this case is true.

\phantomsection\label{annotated-cell-121}%
\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{flights }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ flights }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{1}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{distance\_cat =} \FunctionTok{case\_when}\NormalTok{( }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{2}}
\NormalTok{    distance }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{400} \SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}} \StringTok{"short"}\NormalTok{, }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{3}}
\NormalTok{    distance }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{400} \SpecialCharTok{\&}\NormalTok{ distance }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{1000} \SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}} \StringTok{"medium"}\NormalTok{, }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{4}}
\NormalTok{    distance }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{1000} \SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}} \StringTok{"long"}\NormalTok{) }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{5}}
\NormalTok{  )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\circled{1}]
Take the \texttt{flights} data set, and then,
\item[\circled{2}]
Create a new variable called \texttt{distance\_cat} where
\item[\circled{3}]
if the \texttt{distance}of the flight is less than or equal to 400
miles, set the value of \texttt{distance\_cat} to ``short''
\item[\circled{4}]
if the \texttt{distance} is between 400 and 1000 miles, set the value of
\texttt{distance\_cat} to ``medium''
\item[\circled{5}]
if the \texttt{distance} is over 1000 miles, set the value of
\texttt{distance\_cat} to ``long''.
\end{description}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Trust but verify}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Create a grouped boxplot of \texttt{distance} against
\texttt{distance\_cat} to confirm that this recode worked.

Solution

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ggpubr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{ggboxplot}\NormalTok{(flights, }\AttributeTok{y=}\StringTok{"distance"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"distance\_cat"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{dplyr_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-22-1.pdf}}

\end{tcolorbox}

This \texttt{case\_when} function can be used with complex logical
statements involving multiple variables. Additionally, you don't have to
specify every single possible combiantion. If none of the statments
specified evaluate as TRUE, the \texttt{.default} value will be used.

\phantomsection\label{annotated-cell-122}%
\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{flights }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ flights }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{1}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{delay\_type =} \FunctionTok{case\_when}\NormalTok{( }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{2}}
\NormalTok{             dep\_delay }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0} \SpecialCharTok{\&}\NormalTok{ arr\_delay }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0} \SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}} \StringTok{"two delays"}\NormalTok{, }
\NormalTok{             dep\_delay }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{0} \SpecialCharTok{\&}\NormalTok{ arr\_delay }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{0} \SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}} \StringTok{"no delays"}\NormalTok{, }
             \ConstantTok{TRUE} \SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}} \StringTok{"one delay"} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{3}}
\NormalTok{           )}
\NormalTok{  )}
\FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(flights}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{delay\_type, }\AttributeTok{useNA=}\StringTok{"always"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\circled{1}]
Take the \texttt{flights} data set, and then,
\item[\circled{2}]
Create a new variable called \texttt{delay\_type} based on whether or
not there was a delay at both sides (departure and arrival), if there
was no delays either departing or arriving
\item[\circled{3}]
If neither of these two statments are true, there is a delay at either
the departure or arrival and so we set \texttt{delay\_type} to ``one
delay''.
\end{description}

\begin{verbatim}

 no delays  one delay two delays       <NA> 
    158900      85573      92303          0 
\end{verbatim}

\section{Grouped Operations}\label{grouped-operations}

The above verbs are useful, but they become really powerful when you
combine them with the idea of ``group by'', repeating the operation
individually on groups of observations within the dataset. In dplyr, you
use the \texttt{group\_by()} function to describe how to break a dataset
down into groups of rows. You can then use the resulting object in
exactly the same functions as above; they'll automatically work ``by
group'' when the input is a grouped.

Let's demonstrate how some of these functions work after grouping the
flights data set by month. First we'll create a new data set that is
grouped by month.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{by\_month }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(flights, month)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{summarise()} verb allows you to calculate summary statistics
for each group. This is probably the most common function that is used
in conjunction with \texttt{group\_by}. For example, the average
distance flown per month.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(by\_month, }\AttributeTok{avg\_airtime =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(distance, }\AttributeTok{na.rm=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 12 x 2
   month avg_airtime
   <int>       <dbl>
 1     1       1007.
 2     2       1001.
 3     3       1012.
 4     4       1039.
 5     5       1041.
 6     6       1057.
 7     7       1059.
 8     8       1062.
 9     9       1041.
10    10       1039.
11    11       1050.
12    12       1065.
\end{verbatim}

Or simply the total number of flights per month,

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{summarize}\NormalTok{(by\_month, }\AttributeTok{count=}\FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{())}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 12 x 2
   month count
   <int> <int>
 1     1 27004
 2     2 24951
 3     3 28834
 4     4 28330
 5     5 28796
 6     6 28243
 7     7 29425
 8     8 29327
 9     9 27574
10    10 28889
11    11 27268
12    12 28135
\end{verbatim}

Or even more interstingly, the average departure delay, number of
flights with delays, and the maximum length of delay at each of the
three origin airports.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{by\_airport }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(flights, origin)}
\FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(by\_airport, }
          \AttributeTok{ave\_departure\_delay =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(dep\_delay, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{), }
          \AttributeTok{n\_flights\_with\_delay =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(dep\_delay)}\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{), }
          \AttributeTok{max\_depart\_delay =} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(dep\_delay, }\AttributeTok{na.rm=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 3 x 4
  origin ave_departure_delay n_flights_with_delay max_depart_delay
  <chr>                <dbl>                <int>            <dbl>
1 EWR                   15.1               117596             1126
2 JFK                   12.1               109416             1301
3 LGA                   10.3               101509              911
\end{verbatim}

Seems like Laguardia (LGA) is the better airport for the least delays.

\section{Chaining Operations}\label{chaining-operations}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Same pipe, different symbol}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

We've seen chaining before using the base R pipe
\texttt{\textbar{}\textgreater{}}. The \texttt{\%\textgreater{}\%}
symbol is also called a pipe, but is only accessible after you load the
\texttt{dplyr} package. They function the same. We show both here
because you will see both used out in the wild and need to know they are
interchangeable.

\end{tcolorbox}

Consider the following group of operations that take the data set
\texttt{flights}, and produce a final data set (\texttt{a4}) that
contains only the flights where the daily average delay is greater than
a half hour.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{a1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(flights, year, month, day)}
\NormalTok{a2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(a1, arr\_delay, dep\_delay)}
\NormalTok{a3 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(a2,}
                \AttributeTok{arr =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(arr\_delay, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{),}
                \AttributeTok{dep =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(dep\_delay, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{a4 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(a3, arr }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{30} \SpecialCharTok{|}\NormalTok{ dep }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{head}\NormalTok{(a4)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 6 x 5
# Groups:   year, month [3]
   year month   day   arr   dep
  <int> <int> <int> <dbl> <dbl>
1  2013     1    16  34.2  24.6
2  2013     1    31  32.6  28.7
3  2013     2    11  36.3  39.1
4  2013     2    27  31.3  37.8
5  2013     3     8  85.9  83.5
6  2013     3    18  41.3  30.1
\end{verbatim}

It does the trick, but what if you don't want to save all the
intermediate results (\texttt{a1} - \texttt{a3})? Well these verbs are
\texttt{function}, so they can be wrapped inside other functions to
create a nesting type structure.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(}
      \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(flights, year, month, day),}
\NormalTok{      arr\_delay, dep\_delay}
\NormalTok{    ),}
    \AttributeTok{arr =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(arr\_delay, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{dep =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(dep\_delay, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  ),}
\NormalTok{  arr }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{30} \SpecialCharTok{|}\NormalTok{ dep }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{30}
\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Woah, that is HARD to read! This is difficult to read because the order
of the operations is from inside to out, and the arguments are a long
way away from the function. To get around this problem, dplyr provides
the \texttt{\%\textgreater{}\%} operator.
\texttt{x\ \%\textgreater{}\%\ f(y)} turns into \texttt{f(x,\ y)} so you
can use it to rewrite multiple operations so you can read from
left-to-right, top-to-bottom:

\phantomsection\label{annotated-cell-129}%
\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{flights }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{1}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(year, month, day) }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{2}}
  \FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(arr\_delay, dep\_delay) }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{3}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{( }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{4}}
    \AttributeTok{arr =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(arr\_delay, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{), }
    \AttributeTok{dep =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(dep\_delay, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(arr }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{30} \SpecialCharTok{|}\NormalTok{ dep }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{) }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{5}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\circled{1}]
Take the \texttt{flights} data set and then,
\item[\circled{2}]
\texttt{group} it by date and then,
\item[\circled{3}]
\texttt{select}s the necessary variables, and then,
\item[\circled{4}]
calculate the mean arrival and departure delays, and then,
\item[\circled{5}]
\texttt{filter} to only keep rows with a daily average delay over half
hour.
\end{description}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 49 x 5
# Groups:   year, month [11]
    year month   day   arr   dep
   <int> <int> <int> <dbl> <dbl>
 1  2013     1    16  34.2  24.6
 2  2013     1    31  32.6  28.7
 3  2013     2    11  36.3  39.1
 4  2013     2    27  31.3  37.8
 5  2013     3     8  85.9  83.5
 6  2013     3    18  41.3  30.1
 7  2013     4    10  38.4  33.0
 8  2013     4    12  36.0  34.8
 9  2013     4    18  36.0  34.9
10  2013     4    19  47.9  46.1
# i 39 more rows
\end{verbatim}

The same 4 steps that resulted in the \texttt{a4} data set, but without
all the intermediate data saved! This can be \textbf{very important}
when dealing with Big Data. \texttt{R} stores all data in memory, so if
your little computer only has 2G of RAM and you're working with a data
set that is 500M in size, your computers memory will be used up fast.
\texttt{a1} takes 500M, \texttt{a2} another 500M, by now your computer
is getting slow. Make another copy at \texttt{a3} and it gets worse,
\texttt{a4} now likely won't even be able to be created because you'll
be out of memory.

\chapter{Getting data into R}\label{sec-import}

In this lesson we will introduce methods to get data from external files
into R.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={🎓 Learning Objectives}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

After completing this lesson learners will be able to

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Distinguish between an \texttt{absolute} and \texttt{relative} path,
  and identify which one is used from within an R Project.
\item
  Import data that is stored in an external Excel, CSV or tab-delimited
  text file into R.
\item
  Compare and contrast the three file types discussed in this lesson.
\item
  Use functions to examine data objects to ensure data was imported
  correctly.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Prepare}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Open your Math 130 R Project.
\item
  Right click and ``save as'' this lessons
  \href{notes/import_notes.qmd}{{[}Quarto notes file{]}} and save into
  your \texttt{Math130/notes} folder.
\item
  Right click each link and save the following data sets into your
  \texttt{Math130/data} folder.
\item
  In the \emph{Files} pane, open this Quarto file and Render this file.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{itemize}

  \href{https://www.norcalbiostat.com/data/countyComplete.csv}{County
  Complete}
\item
  \href{https://www.norcalbiostat.com/data/email.txt}{email}
\item
  \href{https://www.norcalbiostat.com/data/fatal-police-shootings-data.xlsx}{Police
  Shootings}
