\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{DO NOT RENAME ANYTHING}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

If you download multiple copies of the data, do not keep the versions
that have a (1) or some number in the name. Do not leave these files in
your downloads folder.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{CAUTION FOR MAC USERS}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Be sure you download the CSV file as .csv and not as a .numbers file. If
you create a file in Numbers, you can choose File \textgreater{} Export
To \textgreater{} CSV to convert it.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{File types}\label{file-types}

In this lesson we are only going to explore reading files that exist on
your computer into R from three most commonly used data sources: A
tab-delimited text file, A CSV file and an Excel file.

The three different files we will be using have different file types, or
extensions. {\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}.doc (Microsoft word), .pdf
(PDF) and .png (images) are examples of file extensions for other types
of files\end{footnotesize}}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{email.txt} is a \texttt{.txt} or ``text'' file.
\item
  \texttt{NCBirths.csv} is a \texttt{.csv} or ``comma separated values''
  file.
\item
  \texttt{fatal-police-shootings-data.xlsx} is a \texttt{.xlsx} or Excel
  file.
\end{itemize}

Each of these file types differ in the type of \emph{delimiter} used.
The \emph{delimiter} is a character or symbol that separates columns of
data from each other when stored in an external file. Recall back to the
earlier lesson on data frames and matrices. Each column in the matrix
represented data on a specific variable. Something had to tell R how to
distinguish which values went with which variable.

There are two main types of delimiters we will consider in this class;
comma and tab. That does not mean that data can't be stored in other
ways, these are just the two most common.

\begin{figure}

\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/tab_sep.png}}

}

\subcaption{\label{fig-tab_sep}Tab separated data. Variable names are
\texttt{id}, \texttt{gender}, \texttt{race}, and \texttt{ses}.}

\end{minipage}%
%
\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/comma_sep.png}}

}

\subcaption{\label{fig-csv}Comma separated data. Variable names are
\texttt{Month}, \texttt{RsnStop}, \texttt{OffRace}, etc}

\end{minipage}%

\caption{\label{fig-delim}Example of two common types of delimiters.}

\end{figure}%

Each delimiter type requires a different function or mechanism to import
the data into R. If you use the wrong mechanism, the data may not be
read in correctly if at all.

\section{Paths (where the data lives)}\label{paths-where-the-data-lives}

In addition to using the correct function for the delimiter type, we
have to then tell the program explicitly where to find the files you
want to access. To do that we need to find the file's \emph{path}. The
\emph{path} is a programmatic way to direct your computer to the
location of a file.

You can think of it as a file's address, or where it lives on your
computer. But to get to that address depends on where you start your
journey. For example to get to your instructors office in Holt Hall, it
will be different depending on if you start from your house, vs start
from Meriam Library. If someone else starts from their house, their path
would look different than if you both started at the Library.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Absolute paths} point to where a file is on a specific
  computer.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Example:
    \texttt{/Users/rdonatello/Library/CloudStorage/Box-Box/Teaching/MATH\ 130/data}
    This path to the \texttt{data} folder only exists on Dr.~Donatello's
    macbook.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Relative paths} indicate where inside the project folder a
  file is located.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Example: \texttt{MATH\ 130/data}. This path to the \texttt{data}
    folder once you're already in the \texttt{MATH\ 130} folder. Anyone
    that has a \texttt{MATH\ 130} folder with a \texttt{data} subfolder
    has the same path.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

When we use R Projects, we are setting the starting point (called the
\emph{working directory}) to your Math 130 folder. We will also
reinforce that by using the \texttt{here} package. We want to specify
that all our paths start \texttt{"here"}, at the top of our project
working directory. {\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}learn more about the
\href{https://here.r-lib.org/articles/here.html}{here}
package\end{footnotesize}}}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{img/here.png}

}

\caption{Cartoon showing two paths side-by-side. On the left is a scary
spooky forest, with spiderwebs and gnarled trees, with file paths
written on the branches like ``\textasciitilde/mmm/nope.csv'' and
``setwd(``/haha/good/luck/''), with a scared looking cute fuzzy monster
running out of it. On the right is a bright, colorful path with flowers,
rainbow and sunshine, with signs saying ``here!'' and ``it's all right
here!'' A monster facing away from us in a backpack and walking stick is
looking toward the right path. Stylized text reads ``here: find your
path.''}

\end{figure}%

{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}Learn more about
\href{https://here.r-lib.org/}{here}.\end{footnotesize}}}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Whats your path?}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

In the console, type \texttt{here::here()} to have R show you what your
current working directory is at. This is the \emph{absolute path} to
your Math 130 folder on your computer.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Dr. D\textquotesingle{}s path to the code to build this course website}
\NormalTok{here}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{here}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "/Users/rdonatello/GitHub/Math130book"
\end{verbatim}

\end{tcolorbox}

Working with a combination of R projects and the \texttt{here} package
this way makes it a lot easier to move your project around on your
computer and share it with others without having to directly modify file
paths in the individual scripts.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Cannot open the Connection}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

The import code in this lesson assumes that you are working in your R
project, and that you have your data downloaded and in the \texttt{data}
folder. If you get an error message saying
\texttt{Cannot\ open\ the\ connection} - double check the following
common places for mistakes:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  is the data file in the right place?
\item
  did you spell the name of the data set correctly?
\item
  are you in your R project and using the \texttt{here::here} function
  correctly?
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Checking the import was
successful}\label{checking-the-import-was-successful}

The first thing you should always do after importing a data file is look
at the raw data and ask yourself the following things:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Were the variable names read in correctly?
\item
  Is there the expected number of rows and columns?
\item
  Are the data types for each variable as expected?
\end{enumerate}

Next we will read in three different data sets, each with unique file
types, and look at each one to make sure it was read in correctly.

\section{Text files}\label{text-files}

Text files are very simple files that have a \texttt{.txt} file
extension. Common delimiters include a space, a comma (,) or a tab.
Uncommon delimiters could include a \texttt{\%} or even a semi-colon
(\texttt{;}).

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Open the file directly}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Navigate to your class folder, and open the \texttt{emamil.txt} file
that should be in your \texttt{data} folder by double clicking. If your
computer asks, you can open it with the \texttt{notepad} program.
Identify the following:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  what's in the first row - data or variable names?
\item
  what is the delimiter? - in this case it is a tab.
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

We will use the \texttt{read.table()} function that is in base R to read
in any type of delimited file.

\phantomsection\label{annotated-cell-130}%
\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{email }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read.table}\NormalTok{( }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{1}}
\NormalTok{          here}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{here}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"data/email.txt"}\NormalTok{), }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{2}}
          \AttributeTok{header=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{, }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{3}}
          \AttributeTok{sep=}\StringTok{"}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{"} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{4}}
\NormalTok{          )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\circled{1}]
Use the \texttt{read.table()} function because it is a text file
\item[\circled{2}]
Use \texttt{here::here} to ensure you start at the project directory,
then go into the \texttt{data} folder, and find the \texttt{email.txt}
data set.
\item[\circled{3}]
Set \texttt{header=TRUE} to signify that the data in the first row
contains our column names
\item[\circled{4}]
Specify that the delimiter is a tab (\texttt{"\textbackslash{}t"}).
\end{description}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Trust but verify}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Let's use the \texttt{head} function to look at the imported data,
ensuring the variable names are intended, and that each column has data
in it.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{head}\NormalTok{(email)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
  spam to_multiple from cc sent_email                time image attach dollar
1    0           0    1  0          0 2011-12-31 22:16:41     0      0      0
2    0           0    1  0          0 2011-12-31 23:03:59     0      0      0
3    0           0    1  0          0 2012-01-01 08:00:32     0      0      4
4    0           0    1  0          0 2012-01-01 01:09:49     0      0      0
5    0           0    1  0          0 2012-01-01 02:00:01     0      0      0
6    0           0    1  0          0 2012-01-01 02:04:46     0      0      0
  winner inherit viagra password num_char line_breaks format re_subj
1     no       0      0        0   11.370         202      1       0
2     no       0      0        0   10.504         202      1       0
3     no       1      0        0    7.773         192      1       0
4     no       0      0        0   13.256         255      1       0
5     no       0      0        2    1.231          29      0       0
6     no       0      0        2    1.091          25      0       0
  exclaim_subj urgent_subj exclaim_mess number
1            0           0            0    big
2            0           0            1  small
3            0           0            6  small

5            0           0            1   none
6            0           0            1   none
\end{verbatim}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Errors!}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

What happens if you forget to include the arguments for \texttt{header}
or \texttt{sep}? Try that now and discuss what happened.

Solution - Forgetting both arguments

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{email.noboth }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read.table}\NormalTok{(here}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{here}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"data/email.txt"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Error in read.table(here::here("data/email.txt")): duplicate 'row.names' are not allowed
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{head}\NormalTok{(email.noboth)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Error: object 'email.noboth' not found
\end{verbatim}

The data set won't successfully be read in, and won't show up in your
environment.

Solution - Forgetting the header

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{email.nohead }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read.table}\NormalTok{(here}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{here}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"data/email.txt"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{sep=}\StringTok{"}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{head}\NormalTok{(email.nohead)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
    V1          V2   V3 V4         V5                  V6    V7     V8     V9
1 spam to_multiple from cc sent_email                time image attach dollar
2    0           0    1  0          0 2011-12-31 22:16:41     0      0      0
3    0           0    1  0          0 2011-12-31 23:03:59     0      0      0
4    0           0    1  0          0 2012-01-01 08:00:32     0      0      4
5    0           0    1  0          0 2012-01-01 01:09:49     0      0      0
6    0           0    1  0          0 2012-01-01 02:00:01     0      0      0
     V10     V11    V12      V13      V14         V15    V16     V17
1 winner inherit viagra password num_char line_breaks format re_subj
2     no       0      0        0    11.37         202      1       0
3     no       0      0        0   10.504         202      1       0
4     no       1      0        0    7.773         192      1       0
5     no       0      0        0   13.256         255      1       0
6     no       0      0        2    1.231          29      0       0
           V18         V19          V20    V21
1 exclaim_subj urgent_subj exclaim_mess number
2            0           0            0    big
3            0           0            1  small
4            0           0            6  small
5            0           0           48  small
6            0           0            1   none
\end{verbatim}

The data is read into the object \texttt{email.nohead}, but the variable
names were read in as datta in the first row. R assigned generic
\texttt{V1}, and \texttt{V2} data names as your clue.

Solution - Forgetting the separator

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{email.nosep }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read.table}\NormalTok{(here}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{here}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"data/email.txt"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{header=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Error in read.table(here::here("data/email.txt"), header = TRUE): duplicate 'row.names' are not allowed
\end{verbatim}

We get the same error message and it will not read the data into your
environment.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{CSV: Comma Separated Values}\label{csv-comma-separated-values}

CSV stands for ``comma-separated values''. This file type can also open
in spreadsheet programs like MS Excel, Numbers or google sheets. The
icons even look similar. Here we use the \texttt{read.csv()} function
because it is optimized to read in this file type.
{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}Not CVS the
pharmacy\end{footnotesize}}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{cc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read.csv}\NormalTok{(here}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{here}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"data/countyComplete.csv"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{header=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{cc[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{] }\CommentTok{\# peek at the first 5 rows and 6 columns of the data}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
            name   state FIPS pop2010 pop2000 age_under_5
1 Autauga County Alabama 1001   54571   43671         6.6
2 Baldwin County Alabama 1003  182265  140415         6.1
3 Barbour County Alabama 1005   27457   29038         6.2
4    Bibb County Alabama 1007   22915   20826         6.0
5  Blount County Alabama 1009   57322   51024         6.3
\end{verbatim}

The variable names are present, the data are all in columns with
reasonable data types.

\section{Excel files}\label{excel-files}

Files with the \texttt{.xlsx} or \texttt{.xls} extensions are Microsoft
Excel files. These are also comma separated, but have more features than
a \texttt{.csv} file. We will use the \texttt{read\_excel()} function
from the \href{http://readxl.tidyverse.org/}{\texttt{readxl} package},
which is contained within the \texttt{tidyverse} package that we
installed earlier.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(readxl)}
\NormalTok{police  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_excel}\NormalTok{(here}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{here}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"data/fatal{-}police{-}shootings{-}data.xlsx"}\NormalTok{), }
                      \AttributeTok{sheet=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col\_names=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{police[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{] }\CommentTok{\# peek at the first 5 rows and 6 columns of the data}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
# A tibble: 6 x 5
  name               date                manner_of_death  armed        age
  <chr>              <dttm>              <chr>            <chr>      <dbl>
1 Tim Elliot         2015-01-02 00:00:00 shot             gun           53
2 Lewis Lee Lembke   2015-01-02 00:00:00 shot             gun           47
3 John Paul Quintero 2015-01-03 00:00:00 shot and Tasered unarmed       23
4 Matthew Hoffman    2015-01-04 00:00:00 shot             toy weapon    32
5 Michael Rodriguez  2015-01-04 00:00:00 shot             nail gun      39
6 Kenneth Joe Brown  2015-01-04 00:00:00 shot             gun           18
\end{verbatim}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Notice this was read in as a \texttt{tibble}, not a
  \texttt{data.frame}. Ref Section Section~\ref{sec-tibble} to recall
  the differences.
\item
  The variable \texttt{date} is a \texttt{dttm} or \emph{date-time}
  variable. This means \texttt{R} recognizes it directly as a date, not
  some string of numbers. This is good.
\item
  Categorical variables such as \texttt{manner\_of\_death} and
  \texttt{city} are read in as \texttt{character} instead of factor. We
  may or may not want to change that later.
\item
  Numeric variables such as \texttt{age} are of type \texttt{dbl}
  (double). This is similar to \texttt{integer} or \texttt{numeric}, so
  we are fine.
\end{itemize}

\part{Advanced Features}

\chapter{Plot Enhancements}\label{sec-plots2}

Section Chapter~\ref{sec-plots1} introduced the main plotting packages
\texttt{ggplot2}, \texttt{ggpubr}, and \texttt{sjPlot} and focused on
creating the correct graphics for the data types. This lesson expands on
those tools and demonstrates how to customize some features and add
enhancements to clarify or to add information to a plot.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={🎓 Learning Objectives}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

After completing this lesson students will be able to

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Change the color and shape of geometries in a plot
\item
  Layer on multiple types of geometries onto a single plot
\item
  Create a grid/panel of plots to create multivarible plots
\item
  Adjust themes, legends, and titles
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Too much to memorize!}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

There is \emph{NO} need to remember all of these adjustments when there
are incredibly handy reference guides and ``cookbooks'' available.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  https://r-graphics.org/
\item
  https://r-graph-gallery.com
\item
  https://ggplot2.tidyverse.org/reference/index.html
\item
  https://r-charts.com
\end{itemize}

This lesson also has no specific order, but is a smattering of ``things
you should know''.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title={👉 Prepare}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Open your Math 130 R Project.
\item
  Right click and ``save as'' this lessons
  \href{notes/plots2_notes.qmd}{{[}Quarto notes file{]}} and save into
  your \texttt{Math130/notes} folder.
\item
  In the \emph{Files} pane, open this Quarto file and Render this file.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(tidyverse) }\CommentTok{\# ggplot, forcats}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(ggpubr)}
\NormalTok{email }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ openintro}\SpecialCharTok{::}\NormalTok{email}
\NormalTok{NCbirths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ openintro}\SpecialCharTok{::}\NormalTok{ncbirths}
\NormalTok{pen }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ palmerpenguins}\SpecialCharTok{::}\NormalTok{penguins}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{tcolorbox}

This lesson uses several data sets that we have seen in prior lessons.

\section{Reorder barcharts in decreasing
order}\label{reorder-barcharts-in-decreasing-order}

We can reorder these levels on the fly so they are being shown in
decreasing frequency using the \texttt{fct\_infreq} function from the
\texttt{forcats} library. You can add this layer to both \texttt{ggplot}
and \texttt{ggpubr} creations.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{x=}\FunctionTok{fct\_infreq}\NormalTok{(island))) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{xlab}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Island"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-2-1.pdf}}

\section{Flipping the axis}\label{flipping-the-axis}

Sometimes barcharts and boxplots look better horizontally, especially
when the category labels are long, but the default is vertical. We can
add a \texttt{coord\_flip} layer to nearly any plot to rotate it 90*.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{island)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+}  
  \FunctionTok{coord\_flip}\NormalTok{()}
\FunctionTok{ggboxplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{y=} \StringTok{"body\_mass\_g"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"island"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
    \FunctionTok{coord\_flip}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-3-1.pdf}}\end{minipage}%
%
\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-3-2.pdf}}\end{minipage}%

\end{figure}%

\section{A ``better'' Pie chart}\label{a-better-pie-chart}

We need to pre-calculate the percentages first, then plot those summary
numbers. So we will chain functions from \texttt{dplyr} and
\texttt{ggpubr} together.

{\marginnote{\begin{footnotesize}See the
\href{https://r-graph-gallery.com/piechart-ggplot2.html}{R graph
gallery} for how to make a pie chart in
\texttt{ggplot}.\end{footnotesize}}}

\phantomsection\label{annotated-cell-138}%
\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{pen }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{1}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(island) }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{2}}
  \FunctionTok{tally}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{3}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{pct =}\NormalTok{ scales}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{percent}\NormalTok{(n}\SpecialCharTok{/}\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(n)), }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{4}}
         \AttributeTok{labs =} \FunctionTok{paste0}\NormalTok{(island, }\StringTok{"}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{ n="}\NormalTok{, n, }\StringTok{"("}\NormalTok{, pct, }\StringTok{")"}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{5}}
  \FunctionTok{ggpie}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"n"}\NormalTok{, }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{6}}
        \AttributeTok{label =} \StringTok{"labs"}\NormalTok{, }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{7}}
        \AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"island"}\NormalTok{, }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{8}}
        \AttributeTok{lab.pos =} \StringTok{"in"}\NormalTok{) }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{9}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\circled{1}]
Take the \texttt{penguin} data set
\item[\circled{2}]
perform these calculations for each \texttt{island} separately
\item[\circled{3}]
\texttt{tally()} counts the number of records by group (like
\texttt{table}), and stores it in a new variable named \texttt{n} (this
is a default name)
\item[\circled{4}]
create a new variable \texttt{pct} that calculates the percent in each
group by dividing the group count \texttt{n}, by the total number of
records \texttt{sum(n)}, then using the \texttt{percent} function from
the \texttt{scales} package to display this decimal as a percent
\item[\circled{5}]
Create chart labels by \texttt{paste}ing the value of the variable
\texttt{island}, then a line break (new line)
\texttt{\textbackslash{}n}, then the text \texttt{n=}, then the value of
the variable \texttt{n}, followed by the value of the percent variable
\texttt{pct} wrapped in parenthesis
\item[\circled{6}]
pass all of this summarized data into \texttt{ggpie} - the function to
create the pie chart, and specify that the numbers to be plotted is in
the variable \texttt{n}
\item[\circled{7}]
and that the wedge labels are found in the variable \texttt{labs} that
was created in step 5
\item[\circled{8}]
color the wedges based on the \texttt{island} variable
\item[\circled{9}]
and put these labels inside the circle
\end{description}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-4-1.pdf}}

\section{Adding the mean}\label{adding-the-mean}

Adding annotation on a numeric distribution plot to indicate where the
mean is located is often a nice informative feature.

\section{ggplot2}

The mean can be denoted on \texttt{geom\_histogram} or
\texttt{geom\_density} using a vertical line (\texttt{geom\_vline})
where the \texttt{xintercept} is set as the calculated mean of the
variable being plotted.

With boxplots and violins, the code below uses the
\texttt{stat\_summary} layer to add the \texttt{mean} as a summary
\texttt{fun}ction, we plot it as a \texttt{point} on the graph,
\texttt{shape=17} makes it a triangle (see \texttt{?pch} for more
shapes), \texttt{size=4} makes it a bit larger so it stands out. There
are also \texttt{color} and \texttt{fill} options available if desired.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{body\_mass\_g)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_histogram}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_vline}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{xintercept=}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(body\_mass\_g, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)),}
            \AttributeTok{color=}\StringTok{"blue"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{linetype=}\StringTok{"dashed"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{body\_mass\_g, }\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ island)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{stat\_summary}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{fun=}\StringTok{"mean"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{geom=}\StringTok{"point"}\NormalTok{, }
               \AttributeTok{shape=}\DecValTok{17}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{size=}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-5-1.pdf}}

}

\subcaption{using \texttt{geom\_vline} to add to a histogram or density}

\end{figure}%

\end{minipage}%
%
\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-5-2.pdf}}

}

\subcaption{using \texttt{stat\_summary} to add the mean to a boxplot}

\end{figure}%

\end{minipage}%

\end{figure}%

Play around with the \texttt{shape}, \texttt{fill}/\texttt{color}, and
\texttt{size} until you achieve your desired look.

\section{ggpubr}

With \texttt{ggpubr} you can use use the \texttt{add="mean"} argument to
both \texttt{gghistogram} and \texttt{ggdensity} to add a vertical
dashed line to denote the location of the mean.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{gghistogram}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{x=}\StringTok{"body\_mass\_g"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"species"}\NormalTok{,}
            \AttributeTok{add =} \StringTok{"mean"}\NormalTok{,)}
\FunctionTok{ggboxplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{y=}\StringTok{"body\_mass\_g"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"species"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"species"}\NormalTok{, }
          \AttributeTok{add =} \StringTok{"mean\_se"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{add.params =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"white"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-6-1.pdf}}\end{minipage}%
%
\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-6-2.pdf}}\end{minipage}%

\end{figure}%

With \texttt{ggpboxplot}, you can \texttt{add} many summary statistics
such as the mean as a dot and line representing uncertainty such as the
standard deviation (sd) and standard error (se). I also used
\texttt{add.params} to make the color of these means easier to see. See
the \texttt{add} and \texttt{add.params} entries in the help file for
\texttt{?ggboxplot} for more options.

\subsection{Histograms + density curve}\label{histograms-density-curve}

Often it is more helpful to have the density (or kernel density) plot
\emph{on top of} a histogram plot.

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ggplot2}}{ggplot2}}

\phantomsection\label{annotated-cell-359}%
\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{body\_mass\_g)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{1}}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_density}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{col=}\StringTok{"blue"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_histogram}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{..density..), }\AttributeTok{colour=}\StringTok{"black"}\NormalTok{, }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{2}}
                 \AttributeTok{fill=}\ConstantTok{NA}\NormalTok{) }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{3}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\circled{1}]
The syntax starts the same: we'll add a \texttt{geom\_density} and color
the line blue for funsies.
\item[\circled{2}]
Then we add the histogram geom using \texttt{geom\_histogram} but must
specify that the y axis should be on the density, not frequency, scale.
Note that this has to go inside the aesthetic statement \texttt{aes()}.
\item[\circled{3}]
I'm also going to get rid of the fill by using \texttt{NA} so the
colored bars don't plot over the density line.
\end{description}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-7-1.pdf}}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ggpubr}}{ggpubr}}

You start with a \texttt{gghistogram} and then \texttt{add\_denstity} to
the plot. This does not always work well with every variable. Sometimes
the density plot is too small to view.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{gghistogram}\NormalTok{(pen, }\AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"bill\_depth\_mm"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"species"}\NormalTok{, }
            \AttributeTok{add\_density =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-8-1.pdf}}

\section{Adding violins to a boxplot}\label{adding-violins-to-a-boxplot}

A violin plot is like a density plot, turned on its side, and reflected
around the axis for symmetry purposes. Overlaying a boxplot and a violin
plot serves a similar purpose to Histograms + Density plots. It shows
outliers, the location of most the data, and better shows the shape/skew
of the distribution.

\section{ggplot2}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(pen, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{body\_mass\_g, }\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{island, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{island)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
        \FunctionTok{geom\_violin}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{alpha=}\NormalTok{.}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
        \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{alpha=}\NormalTok{.}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{width=}\NormalTok{.}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-9-1.pdf}}

\section{ggpubr}

We start with a \texttt{ggviolin} and then add a \texttt{boxplot}. Note
that I also add \texttt{coord\_flip()} to change the orientation of the
boxplots to horizontal.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggviolin}\NormalTok{(pen, }
  \AttributeTok{x=}\StringTok{"island"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"body\_mass\_g"}\NormalTok{, }
  \AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"island"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{add =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"mean"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"boxplot"}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{coord\_flip}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-10-1.pdf}}

\section{Themes}\label{themes}

The standard theme has a gray background, white grid lines etc. Themes
can be changed by adding \texttt{theme\_X()} where \texttt{X} has
several options

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{line\_breaks, }\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{num\_char)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{theme\_bw}\NormalTok{()}
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{line\_breaks, }\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{num\_char)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{theme\_void}\NormalTok{()}
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{line\_breaks, }\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{num\_char)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{theme\_dark}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

\begin{minipage}{0.33\linewidth}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-11-1.pdf}}

}

\subcaption{theme\_bw()}

\end{figure}%

\end{minipage}%
%
\begin{minipage}{0.33\linewidth}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-11-2.pdf}}

}

\subcaption{theme\_void()}

\end{figure}%

\end{minipage}%
%
\begin{minipage}{0.33\linewidth}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-11-3.pdf}}

}

\subcaption{theme\_dark()}

\end{figure}%

\end{minipage}%

\end{figure}%

Explore tons more themes at \url{https://r-charts.com/ggplot2/themes/}

\section{Legends (keys)}\label{legends-keys}

\subsection{Title of legend}\label{title-of-legend}

Add the \texttt{name=} argument to whatever layer you added that created
the legend. Here I specified a \texttt{fill}, and it was a
\texttt{discrete} variable. So I use the
\texttt{scale\_fill\_discrete()} layer.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{num\_char, }\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{number, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{number)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{scale\_fill\_discrete}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{name=}\StringTok{"Size of number"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-12-1.pdf}}

Here I \texttt{col}or the box outlines so the layer is
\texttt{scale\_color\_discrete()}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{num\_char, }\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{number, }\AttributeTok{col=}\NormalTok{number)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{scale\_color\_discrete}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{name=}\StringTok{"Size of number"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-13-1.pdf}}

\subsection{Moving \& removing the
legend}\label{moving-removing-the-legend}

\section{Moving}

The \texttt{legend.position} can be controlled using a \texttt{theme()}
layer.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{num\_char, }\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{number, }\AttributeTok{col=}\NormalTok{number)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{legend.position =} \StringTok{"top"}\NormalTok{) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-14-1.pdf}}

Note that this involves a call to \texttt{theme()}. If you have apply a
named theme like \texttt{theme\_bw()} \emph{after} setting
legend.position using \texttt{theme}, it will overwrite that
modification. The order of ggplot layers matters.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{num\_char, }\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{number, }\AttributeTok{col=}\NormalTok{number)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{legend.position =} \StringTok{"top"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{theme\_minimal}\NormalTok{()}
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{num\_char, }\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{number, }\AttributeTok{col=}\NormalTok{number)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{theme\_minimal}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{legend.position =} \StringTok{"top"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-15-1.pdf}}\end{minipage}%
%
\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-15-2.pdf}}\end{minipage}%

\end{figure}%

\section{Removing}

Sometimes the legend is not needed. It's providing redundant information
and takes up space, so let's remove it entirely by adding
\texttt{guide="none"} to the \texttt{scale\_} layer.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{num\_char, }\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{number, }\AttributeTok{col=}\NormalTok{number)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{scale\_color\_discrete}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{guide=}\StringTok{"none"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-16-1.pdf}}

\section{Title and axis labels}\label{title-and-axis-labels}

A good plot needs to stand alone and convey as much information as
possible. \texttt{ggplot2} provides a single \texttt{labs} function that
is very versitile and includes options for alt-text as well. See
\texttt{?labs} for more information

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{title} for an overall plot title
\item
  \texttt{y} and \texttt{x} adjust the axis labels.
\item
  using \texttt{\textbackslash{}n} creates a line break
\end{itemize}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{num\_char, }\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{number, }\AttributeTok{col=}\NormalTok{number)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{scale\_color\_discrete}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{guide=}\StringTok{"none"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Distribution of the number of characters in an email }\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{ based on the size of the number in the email"}\NormalTok{, }
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"Size of number in email"}\NormalTok{, }
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Number of characters"}
\NormalTok{  )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-17-1.pdf}}

\section{Changing colors}\label{changing-colors}

Full guide on how to change colors in \texttt{ggplot2} at
\url{https://r-graph-gallery.com/ggplot2-color.html}

\section{Manual}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{number, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{number)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+}
    \FunctionTok{scale\_fill\_manual}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{values=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"red"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"green"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"blue"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-18-1.pdf}}

There's some spectacularly colors available. See
\url{https://r-charts.com/colors/} for the full list. You can also type
\texttt{colors()} in the console.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{number, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{number)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+}
    \FunctionTok{scale\_fill\_manual}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{values=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"salmon"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"slategray4"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"violet"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-19-1.pdf}}

And you can use
\href{https://www.w3schools.com/colors/colors_picker.asp}{hex colors}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{number, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{number)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+}
    \FunctionTok{scale\_fill\_manual}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{values=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"\#47dbff"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"\#b3b3cc"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"\#ff531a"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-20-1.pdf}}

\section{Using a color palette (recommended)}

There are 3 types of palettes:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{\emph{Sequential}} palettes are suited to ordered data that
  progress from low to high. Lightness steps dominate the look of these
  schemes, with light colors for low data values to dark colors for high
  data values.
\item
  \textbf{\emph{Diverging}} palettes put equal emphasis on mid-range
  critical values and extremes at both ends of the data range. The
  critical class or break in the middle of the legend is emphasized with
  light colors and low and high extremes are emphasized with dark colors
  that have contrasting hues.
\item
  \textbf{\emph{Qualitative}} palettes do not imply magnitude
  differences between legend classes, and hues are used to create the
  primary visual differences between classes. Qualitative schemes are
  best suited to representing nominal or categorical data.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{R ColorBrewer}\label{r-colorbrewer}

One of the most common color pallet packages used. See the
\href{https://r-graph-gallery.com/38-rcolorbrewers-palettes.html}{full
set of palettes available}.

The function to use is \texttt{scale\_fill\_brewer} (or
\texttt{scale\_color\_brewer}), with an argument specifying the palette
name in quotes.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(RColorBrewer)}
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{number, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{number)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+}
    \FunctionTok{scale\_fill\_brewer}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{palette =} \StringTok{"Dark2"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-21-1.pdf}}

\subsection{Viridis}\label{viridis}

\texttt{viridis} provide a series of color maps that are designed to
improve graph readability for readers with common forms of color
blindness and/or color vision deficiency.
\href{https://sjmgarnier.github.io/viridisLite/reference/viridis.html}{Eight
scales are available}, but \texttt{magma}, \texttt{inferno},
\texttt{plasma}, and \texttt{viridis} as defined in Matplotlib for
Python. These color maps are designed in such a way that they will
analytically be perfectly perceptually-uniform, both in regular form and
also when converted to black-and-white. They are also designed to be
perceived by readers with the most common form of color blindness.

If you are \texttt{fill}ing or \texttt{col}oring by a categorical
variable, you'll use the corresponding \texttt{scale\_fill\_discrete} or
\texttt{scale\_color\_discrete} function, and then pass the name of the
palette with the number of levels needed as a function to the
\texttt{palette} argument.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(viridisLite)}
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(email, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{number, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{number)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+}
    \FunctionTok{scale\_fill\_discrete}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{palette =} \FunctionTok{magma}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-22-1.pdf}}

\section{Multivariate plotting}\label{multivariate-plotting}

So far we've only looked at how to plot 2 variables on the same plot.
Let's up our game some.

\section{Boxplots with three variables}

In the first plotting lesson, we \texttt{fill}ed the boxes with the same
categorical variable that was on the x axis. We can also fill (or color)
the boxes by a third variable if you want an additional comparison.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(NCbirths, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{weeks, }\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{mature, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{habit)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-23-1.pdf}}

This lets us compare the distribution of gestation period for smokers vs
non smokers, within mature and younger moms.

\section{Scatterplots}

\phantomsection\label{annotated-cell-373}%
\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{NCbirths }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{1}}
  \FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(weeks, weight, habit) }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} 
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(habit)) }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} 
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{weeks, }\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{weight, }\AttributeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ habit)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{2}}
         \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{se=}\ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} 
  \FunctionTok{theme\_minimal}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{scale\_color\_brewer}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{palette =} \StringTok{"Set1"}\NormalTok{) }\hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{3}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\circled{1}]
Starting with the \texttt{NCbirths} data set, I \texttt{select} the
variables i want to plot, and then use \texttt{filter} to drop rows with
missing values for \texttt{habit}.
\item[\circled{2}]
Create a scatterplot for gestation \texttt{weeks} against baby
\texttt{weight}, coloring by the mothers smoking \texttt{habit}. Add
both points and loess lines.
\item[\circled{3}]
Make the graph more visually readable by modifying the theme and adding
a color palette.
\end{description}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-24-1.pdf}}

\section{Faceting / paneling}\label{faceting-paneling}

ggplot introduces yet another term called \texttt{faceting}. The
definition is \emph{a particular aspect or feature of something}, or
\emph{one side of something many-sided, especially of a cut gem}.
Basically instead of plotting the grouped graphics on the same plotting
area, we let each group have it's own plot, or facet.

We add a \texttt{facet\_wrap()} and specify the variable we want to
panel on after the \texttt{\textasciitilde{}} tilde.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(NCbirths, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{mage, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{gender)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_density}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{alpha=}\NormalTok{.}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{facet\_wrap}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{mature)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-25-1.pdf}}

The grid placement can be semi-controlled by using the \texttt{ncol}
argument in the \texttt{facet\_wrap()} statement.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(NCbirths, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{mage, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{gender)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_density}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{alpha=}\NormalTok{.}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{facet\_wrap}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{mature, }\AttributeTok{ncol=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-26-1.pdf}}

It is important to compare distributions across groups on the same
scale, and our eyes can compare items vertically better than
horizontally.

\subsection{Paneling on two variables}\label{paneling-on-two-variables}

Who says we're stuck with only faceting on one variable?
Using\texttt{facet\_grid(} we can specify multiple variables to panel
on.

\phantomsection\label{annotated-cell-145}%
\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{NCbirths }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} \hspace*{\fill}\NormalTok{\circled{1}}
  \FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(weeks, marital, habit, mature) }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} 
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(habit)) }\SpecialCharTok{\%\textgreater{}\%} 
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{weeks, }\AttributeTok{fill=}\NormalTok{mature)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{geom\_density}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{alpha=}\NormalTok{.}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{facet\_grid}\NormalTok{(marital}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{habit)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-27-1.pdf}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Distribution of weeks
\item
  colored by maturity status
\item
  paneled on the combination of smoking habit and marital status
\end{itemize}

\section{Multiple plots per window}\label{multiple-plots-per-window}

We can set the number of columns figures show up in using code chunk
option: \texttt{layout-ncol}. This method works only in Quarto
documents.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\InformationTok{\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}\{r\}}
\InformationTok{\#| layout{-}ncol: 2}
\InformationTok{ggplot(pen, aes(x=body\_mass\_g, fill=species)) + geom\_density(alpha=.2)}
\InformationTok{ggplot(pen, aes(x=body\_mass\_g, col=island)) + geom\_density() }
\InformationTok{\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-28-1.pdf}}\end{minipage}%
%
\begin{minipage}{0.50\linewidth}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{plots2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-28-2.pdf}}\end{minipage}%

\end{figure}%

\chapter*{Seeking Help}\label{seeking-help}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Seeking Help}

\markboth{Seeking Help}{Seeking Help}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/jerrymaguire.gif}}

\section*{Advice on asking for help}\label{advice-on-asking-for-help}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Advice on asking for help}

\markright{Advice on asking for help}

The key to receiving help from someone is for them to rapidly grasp your
problem. You should make it as easy as possible to pinpoint where the
issue might be.

Try to use the correct words to describe your problem. For instance, a
package is not the same thing as a library. Most people will understand
what you meant, but it can make things confusing for people trying to
help you. Be as precise as possible when describing your problem.

Don't let not knowing exactly how to describe your problem prevent you
from asking. Screenshots help tremendously!

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{Do this -}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Explain what thing you are trying to do
\item
  Explain/show the code you wrote to try to do that thing
\item
  Explain/show your result, and if it's not obvious explain why you feel
  it's not the correct result. (E.g. you expected the answer to be 5,
  but instead it's 10. )
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Not this -}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Spend more than 20 minutes being frustrated before you ask for help
\item
  Post a vague message in discord like ``i have an error'' with no other
  context
\item
  Be afraid to ask for help
\item
  Think that your question is ``dumb'' or that ``everyone else gets it''
  (you're not, and no they don't)
\item
  Don't go ask Chat GPT or any other AI tool
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/believe.png}}

}

\caption{Header text ``R learners'' above five friendly monsters holding
up signs that together read ``we believe in you.''}

\end{figure}%

\section*{Using AI tools}\label{using-ai-tools}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Using AI tools}

\markright{Using AI tools}

AI coding tools are not permitted for any stage or phase of work in this
class. You are \emph{just} learning how to program in R, and so there
are a lot of ways AI can make this experience worse, not better.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  It can be wrong in ways you don't understand
\item
  It can give you sloppy, inefficient, and unnecessary code
\item
  It can show you techniques that are \emph{technically} correct
  (e.g.~it comes to the same result), but not in the streamlined way
  that this course teaches
\item
  It doesn't help you learn right now. It's a crutch, and will take you
  so much longer to actually learn what to do.
\end{itemize}

Imagine if you went to another country where you did not speak the
language and relied 100\% on using google translate to get around. Sure
it's mostly fine, and you get your point across most the time, but it
takes much longer, can be more convoluted and anoyying for everyone.
Just take the time to learn the basics.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{You will grow into it}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

I'm \textbf{not} saying that AI can't be useful to help you code. Just
not yet at this stage. Once you have a basic understanding of
fundamentals, can provide clear instructions, and have a better
``bullshit'' radar, AI can be \emph{very} helpful in figuring out how to
code things.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Help from inside R Studio}\label{help-from-inside-r-studio}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Help from inside R Studio}

\markright{Help from inside R Studio}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{I know the name of the function I want to use, but im not sure how to
use it}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Use the built-in RStudio help interface (bottom right corner) to search
for more information on R functions. E.g. \texttt{?mean} or
\texttt{?ggboxplot}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{I want to use a function that does X, there must be a function for it
but I dont know which one\ldots{}}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

If you are looking for a function to do a particular task, you can use
the \texttt{help.search()} function, which is called by the double
question mark \texttt{??}. However, this only looks through the
installed packages for help pages with a match to your search request

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{??kruskal}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If you can't find what you are looking for, you can use the
\href{http://www.rdocumentation.org}{rdocumentation.org} website that
searches through the help files across all packages available.

Finally, a generic Google or internet search ``R
\textless task\textgreater{}'' will often either send you to the
appropriate package documentation or a helpful forum where someone else
has already asked your question.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{I get an error message that I dont understand}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

Start by googling the error message. However, this doesn't always work
very well because often, package developers rely on the error catching
provided by R. You end up with general error messages that might not be
very helpful to diagnose a problem (e.g.~``subscript out of bounds'').
If the message is very generic, you might also include the name of the
function or package you're using in your query.

If you check Stack Overflow, search using the \texttt{{[}r{]}} tag. Most
questions have already been answered, but the challenge is to use the
right words in the search to find the answers:
\url{http://stackoverflow.com/questions/tagged/r}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Development of R moves pretty fast.}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

When at all possible, use results from the past 1-2 years. Anything over
5 years old for packages such as \texttt{ggplot}, \texttt{dplyr}, and
\texttt{forcats} are likely obsolete.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{In Person help}\label{sec-iphelp}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{In Person help}

\markright{In Person help}

\subsection*{Sometimes you just need another set of
eyeballs}\label{sometimes-you-just-need-another-set-of-eyeballs}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Sometimes you just need another set of
eyeballs}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{img/errorgator.png}}

}

\caption{Comic panels of an alligator trying to debug some code. First
panel: A confident looking alligator gets an error message. Second
panel: a few minutes later, the error remains and the alligator is
looking carefully at their code. Third panel: 10 minutes after that, the
error remains and the alligator is giving a frustrated ``RAAAR'' while
desperately typing. Fourth panel: The error remains, and the alligator
looks exhausted and exasperated, and a thought bubble reads ``maybe it's
a bug.'' Fifth panel: A friendly flamingo comes over to take a look, and
reads aloud from the problematic code a spelling error: ``L-E-N-G-H-T.''
Only the tail of the alligator is visible as it stomp stomp stomps out
of the panel roaring.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Start by ask the person sitting next to you! Don't hesitate to talk to
  your neighbor, compare your answers, and ask for help.
\item
  Ask in the Math \& Statistics
  \href{https://discord.gg/ck497bJty5}{Discord server} - we have a
  dedicated channel just for this class but a lot of students in the
  server know how to use R.
\item
  Attend
  \href{https://datascience.csuchico.edu/communitycoding}{Community
  Coding}.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Drop in work session \& dedicated space to work on coding projects.
  \item
    Collaborate with your peers and learn from experts.
  \item
    Some hours are held over Zoom
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Project}\label{project}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Project}

\markboth{Project}{Project}

Time to put everything you learned in this class into action. In an
exploratory data analysis (EDA) you are just looking at (exploring) the
data and learning about the data and possible relationships between
variables. This is not a formal statistical analysis, you cannot make
any claims about groups being statistically different. This is just
descriptive. You are allowed and encouraged to hypothesize why you
observe certain relationships or data characteristics, just be sure not
to draw any conclusions from the data.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, bottomrule=.15mm, colback=white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, toprule=.15mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, toptitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{See prior examples}, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0]

This is not a research class, and so we suggest that you take a look at
some
\href{https://drive.google.com/drive/folders/1EGyiCnCBocPAM5ouhbPIo0carF53by54}{projects
from prior students} to get an idea of what is expected and what a final
project should look like.

\end{tcolorbox}

\chapter*{Instructions}\label{instructions}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Instructions}

\markboth{Instructions}{Instructions}

Using your data set of choice, pose a brief research question that
explores the relationship between 2-3 variables.

Use markdown headers to make the following sections

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Introduction}: A short introduction/description of the data.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Specifically mention the 2-3 variables you are going to explore.
  \item
    What is your research question? What are you interested in finding
    out more about?
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Univariate Exploration}: Describe each of the variables under
  consideration.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    This means calculate summary statistics appropriate for your data
    type (N(\%) or mean(sd)) and make a graphic
  \item
    Describe what you see in the graphic, and what the numbers say in a
    sentence or two.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Bivariate Exploration}: Comparison between two variables of
  interest.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Calculate grouped summary statistics as appropriate. \emph{This is
    often the most often forgotten part}
  \item
    Create an appropriate bivariate visualization
  \item
    You can go further and explore more than two variables at a time
    using paneling, but be sure to explain what you learn from each
    graph.
  \item
    Describe the patterns you are noticing, with summary numbers, in a
    sentence or two.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Conclusion}: What did you find? If you had a prior hypothesis,
  does the data seem to support it? Remember this is NOT a statistical
  analysis.
\end{enumerate}

This is a very vague set of instructions for a reason. I want you to
explore and choose a pair of variables that you find interesting. Create
tables, graphics, grouped summary statistics (mean of the continuous
variable across levels of the categorical variable). Whatever you need
to do to understand the relationship between these two measures.

Use the grading rubric at the end of this document for guidance as to
what you should present, in what order, and level of detail you need to
present.

\section*{Data}\label{data}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Data}

\markright{Data}

You have a choice here. If you are currently working on some data that
you would like to explore, talk with your instructor to get your data
set approved. As long as it has more than 4 variables in it, and at
least 30 observations it should be fine.

If you do not have your own data, you can choose from one of the
following data sets, all of which can be downloaded from the
\href{https://www.norcalbiostat.com/data/}{Data} page of Dr.~D's
teaching course website.

Note that you can't use the following data sets because we've used them
too much already: \texttt{diamonds}, \texttt{NCbirths}, \texttt{email},
or \texttt{penguins}.

\chapter*{Guidelines}\label{guidelines}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Guidelines}

\markboth{Guidelines}{Guidelines}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Render early and often. As often as every time you include a new R
  code chunk.
\item
  Spell check your report prior to submission using RStudio.
\item
  Re-read your report and edit for clarification and removing duplicated
  information.
\item
  Remove superfluous code and output (i.e.~printing a data set to the
  screen).
\item
  This is to be independent work. Papers that are too similar will
  receive no credit.
\item
  Look at the grading rubric to help you decide the level of detail
  required.
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Peer Review}\label{peer-review}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Peer Review}

\markboth{Peer Review}{Peer Review}

After the submission deadline, your analysis projects will be randomly
assigned to two other people to peer review and score. This means you
will also score and provide feedback on 2 reports. Your instructor will
also score all projects for your class section.

\href{https://math130.netlify.app/pr_rad.qmd}{See here on how to do peer
reviews for Dr.~D}

\chapter*{Grading}\label{grading}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Grading}

\markboth{Grading}{Grading}

The criteria below is what you will be graded on. Below each criteria is
an example of the points awarded for the level of competency. Use this
criteria when you score your peers reports.

\subsubsection*{1. Data Description}\label{data-description}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{1. Data Description}

Provide a description of the data set and the variables of interest.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  (Novice) There is no description or the description is a copy of the
  help file.
\item
  (Competent) There is a minor description of the data but not enough to
  understand what is being measured or compared.
\item
  (Proficient) The data description is clear and concise, it is clear to
  me what data is being analyzed and where it was obtained.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{2. Univariate Description}\label{univariate-description}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{2. Univariate Description}

Fully describe the distribution of \emph{each variable} by itself

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  (Novice) There are no numerical or graphical summaries provided.
\item
  (Competent) Only numeric or only graphical summaries were created, but
  no textual description.
\item
  (Proficient) The variable was fully described using both numeric and
  graphical summary methods.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{3. Bivariate Comparison}\label{bivariate-comparison}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{3. Bivariate Comparison}

Describe the relationship between the two chosen variables.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  (Novice) No comparison was made, or the variables were compared, but
  inappropriate graphics or summary statistics were created.
\item
  (Competent) The variables were compared using appropriate graphical
  methods and grouped summary statistics were created, but nothing was
  discussed.
\item
  (Proficient) The variables were compared using appropriate graphical
  methods and a short textual explanation of what the summaries showed.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{4. Organization / Grammar}\label{organization-grammar}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{4. Organization / Grammar}

How well does the report read? How well organized is it? Was it checked
for grammar and spelling mistakes?

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  (Novice) Only R code, output is present. There is no discussion of
  results. Tons of extra R code that is not relevant to the discussion
  is present. Markdown headers were not used.
\item
  (Competent) An attempt was made to discuss the results, but the
  explanations are not in a report format or there are some large
  grammar and/or spelling problems. Some R code that is not relevant to
  the analysis question at hand is being displayed. Markdown headers
  were used to create sections.
\item
  (Proficient) The report was spell written in well edited, full English
  sentences, and spell checked prior to submission. The report flowed
  well and followed the required order of discussion topics with
  markdown headers used successfully.
\end{itemize}




\end{document}
